Manual review of generated question quality

717 questions read individually across 40 sampled documents and 100 document/mode comparisons. Original, revised and simple labels and previous verifier scores were hidden during review. No new generation or verifier calls were used.

Agent judgment on a stratified sample, not human gold labels or a full review of all 1,778 questions. Reviewers know this project and may recognize previously audited examples. Main scores compare only document/modes where all three versions produced questions. Supplied-answer defects are recorded separately. Labels are revealed below for inspection.

Question ratings: 5 strong as written; 4 good with minor weakness; 3 meaningful editing/context needed or thin/artificial; 2 major repair; 1 invalid/unanswerable. No arbitrary word-limit, fixed-anchor-count or mandatory-date penalties. Legitimate factual extraction and direct application to a hypothetical case are both permitted.

un / lookup174.284.534.32
un / practitioner164.163.984.16
un / semantic184.224.203.88

Average questions within each document/mode first, then average matched documents. The overall summary gives EUR-Lex and UN equal weight; it is not a measure of routing or output coverage.

Overall, equal corpus weighting: original 4.46/5, revised 4.45/5, simple 4.25/5.

Original and Revised are effectively tied on average question quality; Simple is weaker overall. Revised shows its clearest improvement in UN lookup. The native-verifier score increase is not reproduced as an overall question-quality increase in this review.

Best question available in each matched document/mode, using the manual ratings: original 4.74/5, revised 4.83/5, simple 4.72/5. This describes potential with ideal selection, not the quality of the questions the current pipeline actually selects. Revised has more candidates in many UN batches, so best-of-batch comparisons also reflect more chances to produce a strong question.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2014/910/art_41/oj · fact_pattern

Article 41 distinguishes non-discrimination for electronic or non-qualified stamps, evidential presumptions for qualified stamps, and cross-border recognition. All three points are stated directly.

Target source text
Legal effect of electronic time stamps

1. An electronic time stamp shall not be denied legal effect and admissibility as evidence in legal proceedings solely on the grounds that it is in an electronic form or that it does not meet the requirements of the qualified electronic time stamp.

2. A qualified electronic time stamp shall enjoy the presumption of the accuracy of the date and the time it indicates and the integrity of the data to which the date and time are bound.

3. A qualified electronic time stamp issued in one Member State shall be recognised as a qualified electronic time stamp in all Member States.

Reviewer preference: simple, revised. A and C consistently express clear, answerable needs. B remains good but has minor avoidable wording defects; scenario form itself is not penalized.

simple

Three strong questions covering distinct effects; direct form is legitimate.

Question (5/5): What presumption does a qualified electronic time stamp provide concerning the indicated date and time and the integrity of the bound data?

Answer (ok): A qualified electronic time stamp shall enjoy the presumption of the accuracy of the date and the time it indicates and the integrity of the data to which the date and time are bound.

Clearly asks which evidential presumption qualified stamps carry; the source supplies accuracy and integrity.

Question (5/5): Must a qualified electronic time stamp issued in one Member State be recognised as a qualified electronic time stamp in another Member State?

Answer (ok): A qualified electronic time stamp issued in one Member State shall be recognised as a qualified electronic time stamp in all Member States.

A useful and precise cross-border recognition question with an unknown legal outcome.

Question (5/5): Can an electronic time stamp be denied legal effect or admissibility as evidence in legal proceedings solely because it is in electronic form or does not meet the requirements for a qualified electronic time stamp?

Answer (ok): An electronic time stamp shall not be denied legal effect and admissibility as evidence in legal proceedings solely on the grounds that it is in an electronic form or that it does not meet the requirements of the qualified electronic time stamp.

Clearly isolates the prohibited grounds for refusing legal effect or admissibility.

original

All useful; two questions have small wording redundancies or scope looseness.

Question (4/5): A company submits an electronic time stamp in court proceedings, but the time stamp is not a qualified electronic time stamp. May the court reject its legal effect or admissibility solely because it is electronic or does not meet the requirements for a qualified electronic time stamp?

Answer (ok): shall not be denied legal effect and admissibility as evidence in legal proceedings solely on the grounds that it is in an electronic form or that it does not meet the requirements of the qualified electronic time stamp

Answerable and useful, but repeats the non-qualified status and rejection grounds unnecessarily.

Question (4/5): A qualified electronic time stamp was issued in Germany and is now submitted by a company in proceedings in France. How must it be treated in the other Member States?

Answer (ok): shall be recognised as a qualified electronic time stamp in all Member States

Cross-border recognition is answerable; asking about other Member States after naming France leaves the geographic ask slightly loose.

Question (5/5): A party relies in litigation on a qualified electronic time stamp showing when a contract was signed and linking that date and time to the contract data. What evidential presumption applies to the indicated date, time and data?

Answer (ok): shall enjoy the presumption of the accuracy of the date and the time it indicates and the integrity of the data to which the date and time are bound

The contract-evidence scenario gives a clear, useful need for the statutory presumption.

revised

Three clear substantive needs, with valid case facts and source support.

Question (5/5): A qualified electronic time stamp was issued by a trust service provider in Estonia and is submitted in legal proceedings in Italy. How is it to be treated in the other Member State?

Answer (ok): A qualified electronic time stamp issued in one Member State shall be recognised as a qualified electronic time stamp in all Member States.

A concrete cross-border use of a qualified stamp naturally raises its recognition in Italy.

Question (5/5): A German logistics company relies in a payment dispute on a qualified electronic time stamp attached to an electronic delivery record. What is presumed about the date and time shown and the integrity of the linked delivery data?

Answer (ok): A qualified electronic time stamp shall enjoy the presumption of the accuracy of the date and the time it indicates and the integrity of the data to which the date and time are bound.

Delivery evidence in a payment dispute supplies a clear reason to ask about the statutory presumption.

Question (5/5): A Belgian software company submits an electronic time stamp in proceedings before a French court, but the time stamp is not a qualified electronic time stamp. May the court deny it legal effect and admissibility as evidence solely because it is electronic and does not meet the qualified requirements?

Answer (ok): An electronic time stamp shall not be denied legal effect and admissibility as evidence in legal proceedings solely on the grounds that it is in an electronic form or that it does not meet the requirements of the qualified electronic time stamp.

The question clearly isolates electronic and non-qualified status as the sole grounds for refusal.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2014/910/art_41/oj · lookup

Article 41 distinguishes non-discrimination for electronic or non-qualified stamps, evidential presumptions for qualified stamps, and cross-border recognition. All three points are stated directly.

Target source text
Legal effect of electronic time stamps

1. An electronic time stamp shall not be denied legal effect and admissibility as evidence in legal proceedings solely on the grounds that it is in an electronic form or that it does not meet the requirements of the qualified electronic time stamp.

2. A qualified electronic time stamp shall enjoy the presumption of the accuracy of the date and the time it indicates and the integrity of the data to which the date and time are bound.

3. A qualified electronic time stamp issued in one Member State shall be recognised as a qualified electronic time stamp in all Member States.

Reviewer preference: original. A preserves a more genuine unknown in the presumption question; the sets are otherwise closely matched.

original

Useful coverage, with a small semantic looseness in the presumption wording.

Question (5/5): May an electronic time stamp be denied legal effect or admissibility as evidence merely because it is electronic or does not meet the requirements for a qualified electronic time stamp?

Answer (ok): An electronic time stamp shall not be denied legal effect and admissibility as evidence in legal proceedings solely on the grounds that it is in an electronic form or that it does not meet the requirements of the qualified electronic time stamp.

The refusal grounds and requested evidential treatment are precise and directly answered.

Question (4/5): What presumption applies to the date, time and data integrity indicated by a qualified electronic time stamp?

Answer (ok): A qualified electronic time stamp shall enjoy the presumption of the accuracy of the date and the time it indicates and the integrity of the data to which the date and time are bound.

The intended presumption is clear, but data integrity is bound to the data rather than indicated by the stamp in the same sense as its time.

Question (5/5): Must a qualified electronic time stamp issued in one Member State be recognised as such in the other Member States?

Answer (ok): A qualified electronic time stamp issued in one Member State shall be recognised as a qualified electronic time stamp in all Member States.

A precise question about recognition across Member States.

simple

Two strong questions; the presumption question nearly states its own answer.

Question (5/5): How is a qualified electronic time stamp issued in one Member State treated in all other Member States?

Answer (ok): A qualified electronic time stamp issued in one Member State shall be recognised as a qualified electronic time stamp in all Member States.

Clear cross-border recognition need, with the qualified status specified.

Question (3/5): What presumption does a qualified electronic time stamp enjoy concerning the accuracy of its indicated date and time and the integrity of the data to which they are bound?

Answer (ok): A qualified electronic time stamp shall enjoy the presumption of the accuracy of the date and the time it indicates and the integrity of the data to which the date and time are bound.

The question already names accuracy and integrity as the contents of the presumption it asks for; little substantive information remains unknown.

Question (5/5): Can an electronic time stamp be denied legal effect or admissibility as evidence in legal proceedings solely because it is in electronic form or does not meet the requirements of a qualified electronic time stamp?

Answer (ok): An electronic time stamp shall not be denied legal effect and admissibility as evidence in legal proceedings solely on the grounds that it is in an electronic form or that it does not meet the requirements of the qualified electronic time stamp.

Clear and useful question about the two prohibited grounds for refusal.

revised

Two strong questions but one answer-disclosing formulation.

Question (5/5): May an electronic time stamp be denied legal effect or admissibility as evidence in legal proceedings merely because it is electronic or does not meet the requirements of a qualified electronic time stamp?

Answer (ok): An electronic time stamp shall not be denied legal effect and admissibility as evidence in legal proceedings solely on the grounds that it is in an electronic form or that it does not meet the requirements of the qualified electronic time stamp.

Clearly asks whether specified grounds permit refusal of legal effect or admissibility.

Question (3/5): What presumption does a qualified electronic time stamp create concerning the accuracy of its indicated date and time and the integrity of the data to which they are bound?

Answer (ok): A qualified electronic time stamp shall enjoy the presumption of the accuracy of the date and the time it indicates and the integrity of the data to which the date and time are bound.

By naming the presumption of accuracy and integrity in the question, it substantially discloses the requested information.

Question (5/5): Must a qualified electronic time stamp issued in one Member State be recognised as a qualified electronic time stamp in all Member States?

Answer (ok): A qualified electronic time stamp issued in one Member State shall be recognised as a qualified electronic time stamp in all Member States.

Useful and unambiguous cross-border recognition question.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg_impl/2012/282/art_1/oj · fact_pattern

The article identifies four underlying agricultural regimes and regulations adopted under them, with an override where those regulations lay down other rules. It does not describe the substantive security procedures.

Target source text
This Regulation lays down the rules governing securities to be provided, either under the following regulations or under any regulations adopted pursuant to those regulations, unless other rules are laid down by those regulations:

(a)
regulations laying down the common organisation of markets in certain agricultural products:

Regulation (EC) No 104/2000 (fishery and aquaculture products),

Regulation (EC) No 1234/2007 (Single CMO Regulation);

(b)
Regulation (EC) No 73/2009 (direct support schemes);

(c)
Regulation (EC) No 1216/2009 (trade arrangements applicable to certain goods resulting from the processing of agricultural products).

Reviewer preference: original. C sustains useful scope questions across its set; A contains a circular question and B has only one somewhat broad question.

simple

One useful precedence query and one largely circular scope query.

Question (2/5): What do these rules govern for a security required from a private trader under an EU direct-support scheme for an agricultural product?

Answer (major): This Regulation lays down the rules governing securities to be provided, either under the following regulations or under any regulations adopted pursuant to those regulations, unless other rules are laid down by those regulations:

Asks what securities rules govern after already specifying a security; the answer repeats the scope preamble without resolving a substantive need.

Question (4/5): When are the common rules for securities displaced for a private trader filing a security under an EU direct-support scheme for an agricultural product?

Answer (minor): unless other rules are laid down by those regulations

The priority-of-rules issue is useful; EU direct-support scheme could be identified more precisely, and the answer retains an unresolved those regulations.

revised

A useful source-identification question with an inadequate answer; question quality judged separately.

Question (4/5): A fishery cooperative is required to provide a security for a measure concerning fishery and aquaculture products under the common organisation of markets rules. Which EU rules govern that security?

Answer (major): This Regulation lays down the rules governing securities to be provided, either under the following regulations or under any regulations adopted pursuant to those regulations, unless other rules are laid down by those regulations: (a) regulations laying down the common organisation of markets in certain agricultural products: Regulation (EC) No 104/2000 (fishery and aquaculture products)

The fishery-security setting creates a useful question about governing rules, but the answer says This Regulation without identifying the requested rules.

original

Consistently substantive coverage questions with concrete regulated activities.

Question (5/5): A farmer applying for direct support schemes has been asked to provide a security. Is that security within the scope of the common rules governing securities for agricultural products?

Answer (ok): Regulation (EC) No 73/2009 (direct support schemes)

The direct-support security presents a clear scope question rather than assuming the coverage outcome.

Question (5/5): A fishery trader is required to provide a security in connection with the common organisation of markets for fishery and aquaculture products. Do the common rules for agricultural-product securities cover that security?

Answer (ok): Regulation (EC) No 104/2000 (fishery and aquaculture products)

Fishery and aquaculture market security gives a specific, answerable scope question.

Question (4/5): A food manufacturer exporting goods made by processing agricultural products must provide a security for the applicable trade arrangements. Do the common securities rules cover that security?

Answer (ok): Regulation (EC) No 1216/2009 (trade arrangements applicable to certain goods resulting from the processing of agricultural products)

Processed-agricultural-goods security is a useful scope question; the applicable trade arrangements are described somewhat broadly.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg_impl/2012/282/art_1/oj · lookup

The article identifies four underlying agricultural regimes and regulations adopted under them, with an override where those regulations lay down other rules. It does not describe the substantive security procedures.

Target source text
This Regulation lays down the rules governing securities to be provided, either under the following regulations or under any regulations adopted pursuant to those regulations, unless other rules are laid down by those regulations:

(a)
regulations laying down the common organisation of markets in certain agricultural products:

Regulation (EC) No 104/2000 (fishery and aquaculture products),

Regulation (EC) No 1234/2007 (Single CMO Regulation);

(b)
Regulation (EC) No 73/2009 (direct support schemes);

(c)
Regulation (EC) No 1216/2009 (trade arrangements applicable to certain goods resulting from the processing of agricultural products).

Reviewer preference: revised, original. Both sets ask genuine coverage and precedence questions; B has a substantial circularity defect.

revised

Two clear substantive questions covering scope and displacement.

Question (5/5): Which regulations are covered by the common rules for securities for agricultural products?

Answer (minor): Regulation (EC) No 104/2000 (fishery and aquaculture products), Regulation (EC) No 1234/2007 (Single CMO Regulation), Regulation (EC) No 73/2009 (direct support schemes), and Regulation (EC) No 1216/2009 (trade arrangements applicable to certain goods resulting from the processing of agricultural products).

Clear request for the covered regimes; the list omits the additional coverage of regulations adopted pursuant to them.

Question (5/5): When do the common rules for securities for agricultural products give way to rules in regulations adopted pursuant to Regulation (EC) No 104/2000, Regulation (EC) No 1234/2007, Regulation (EC) No 73/2009 or Regulation (EC) No 1216/2009?

Answer (minor): unless other rules are laid down by those regulations

Useful priority question with the relevant underlying acts identified; the extracted answer has an unresolved those regulations.

simple

Mixed: good coverage list, abstract precedence wording, and a circular subject-matter question.

Question (2/5): What subject matter is governed by the rules for securities for agricultural products under the EU agricultural-product securities regime?

Answer (major): This Regulation lays down the rules governing securities to be provided, either under the following regulations or under any regulations adopted pursuant to those regulations, unless other rules are laid down by those regulations:

The requested subject matter is already stated as securities for agricultural products; the answer adds no clear substantive resolution.

Question (5/5): Which underlying EU measures fall within the scope of the rules for securities for agricultural products?

Answer (ok): regulations laying down the common organisation of markets in certain agricultural products: Regulation (EC) No 104/2000 (fishery and aquaculture products), Regulation (EC) No 1234/2007 (Single CMO Regulation); (b) Regulation (EC) No 73/2009 (direct support schemes); (c) Regulation (EC) No 1216/2009 (trade arrangements applicable to certain goods resulting from the processing of agricultural products).

A clear request for the underlying covered measures, answered by their list.

Question (4/5): What qualification applies to the rules for securities for agricultural products where the underlying regulations lay down other rules?

Answer (minor): unless other rules are laid down by those regulations:

The rule-priority issue is answerable, though what qualification applies is an abstract formulation.

original

Two clear and useful questions, with minor answer-completeness issues.

Question (5/5): Which regulations are covered by the rules governing securities for agricultural products?

Answer (minor): Regulation (EC) No 104/2000 (fishery and aquaculture products), Regulation (EC) No 1234/2007 (Single CMO Regulation), Regulation (EC) No 73/2009 (direct support schemes) and Regulation (EC) No 1216/2009 (trade arrangements applicable to certain goods resulting from the processing of agricultural products)

Clear covered-regimes question; the answer omits regulations adopted under the named acts.

Question (5/5): Can the rules governing securities for agricultural products apply when the relevant regulations lay down different rules?

Answer (minor): unless other rules are laid down by those regulations

A useful yes/no test of the displacement rule; the answer needs its antecedent regulations read from the question.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2010/1031/art_48/oj · fact_pattern

For delivery failures outside a clearing or settlement system’s control, later delivery at the earliest opportunity and acceptance are required; that remedy is exclusive. No outside remedies are inferred.

Target source text
Late delivery of the auctioned allowances

1. Where the clearing system or settlement system fails to deliver the whole or part of the auctioned allowances due to circumstances outside its control, the clearing system or settlement system shall deliver the allowances at the earliest opportunity and the successful bidders or their successors in title shall accept delivery at that later date.

2. The remedy provided for in paragraph 1 shall be the sole remedy to which a successful bidder or its successors in title shall be entitled to in case of any failure to deliver auctioned allowances, due to circumstances outside the control of the clearing system or settlement system concerned.

Reviewer preference: original, revised, simple. Question quality is comparably strong across all groups; C’s answer defect is recorded separately and does not lower its question rating.

original

Both concrete questions are clear and source-resolvable.

Question (5/5): A successful bidder received no auctioned allowances after a settlement system failed to deliver them in circumstances outside the system's control. Can the bidder pursue a remedy other than the remedy provided for the delayed delivery?

Answer (ok): The remedy provided for in paragraph 1 shall be the sole remedy to which a successful bidder or its successors in title shall be entitled to in case of any failure to deliver auctioned allowances, due to circumstances outside the control of the clearing system or settlement system concerned

The stipulated cause and bidder status make the question about alternative remedies directly answerable.

Question (5/5): A clearing system failed to deliver part of the auctioned allowances because of circumstances outside its control, and the successful bidder is still awaiting them. What must the clearing system and the bidder do?

Answer (ok): the clearing system or settlement system shall deliver the allowances at the earliest opportunity and the successful bidders or their successors in title shall accept delivery at that later date

The paired duties form one coherent need about handling the same delayed delivery.

revised

Two well-specified questions addressing delivery and exclusivity.

Question (5/5): A successful bidder at an auction of greenhouse gas emission allowances is due to receive part of the allowances, but the settlement system cannot deliver them because of circumstances outside its control. What must happen to the undelivered allowances and the bidder’s acceptance of delivery?

Answer (ok): the settlement system shall deliver the allowances at the earliest opportunity and the successful bidders or their successors in title shall accept delivery at that later date

The emissions-auction setting and uncontrollable cause make both delivery obligations clear and useful.

Question (5/5): A successor in title to a successful bidder has not received any of the auctioned allowances because the clearing system failed to deliver them for reasons outside its control. Can the successor claim a remedy other than the remedy connected with later delivery?

Answer (ok): The remedy provided for in paragraph 1 shall be the sole remedy to which a successful bidder or its successors in title shall be entitled to in case of any failure to deliver auctioned allowances, due to circumstances outside the control of the clearing system or settlement system concerned

Successor status and uncontrollable failure give a precise, substantive question about available remedies.

simple

Strong questions; the first answer fails to name the requested remedy.

Question (5/5): When auctioned allowances are not delivered because of circumstances outside the relevant clearing or settlement system's control, what remedy is available to the successful bidder or its successor in title?

Answer (major): The remedy provided for in paragraph 1 shall be the sole remedy to which a successful bidder or its successors in title shall be entitled to in case of any failure to deliver auctioned allowances, due to circumstances outside the control of the clearing system or settlement system concerned.

A clear request for the available remedy; the answer only calls an unidentified paragraph-1 remedy exclusive without describing it.

Question (5/5): If a clearing or settlement system fails to deliver all or part of auctioned allowances because of circumstances outside its control, what must it do, and what must the successful bidder accept?

Answer (ok): the clearing system or settlement system shall deliver the allowances at the earliest opportunity and the successful bidders or their successors in title shall accept delivery at that later date.

A single coherent delayed-delivery problem asks for the complementary duties the source states.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2010/1031/art_48/oj · lookup

For delivery failures outside a clearing or settlement system’s control, later delivery at the earliest opportunity and acceptance are required; that remedy is exclusive. No outside remedies are inferred.

Target source text
Late delivery of the auctioned allowances

1. Where the clearing system or settlement system fails to deliver the whole or part of the auctioned allowances due to circumstances outside its control, the clearing system or settlement system shall deliver the allowances at the earliest opportunity and the successful bidders or their successors in title shall accept delivery at that later date.

2. The remedy provided for in paragraph 1 shall be the sole remedy to which a successful bidder or its successors in title shall be entitled to in case of any failure to deliver auctioned allowances, due to circumstances outside the control of the clearing system or settlement system concerned.

Reviewer preference: revised, original, simple. All groups contain strong, answerable questions. Different division of the two legal points does not establish a question-quality winner.

revised

Three clear, complementary questions.

Question (5/5): When a clearing system or settlement system cannot deliver auctioned allowances because of circumstances outside its control, by when must it make delivery?

Answer (ok): at the earliest opportunity

Precisely asks the timing of delivery after an uncontrollable failure.

Question (5/5): What must successful bidders or their successors in title do when delivery of auctioned allowances is made later because of circumstances outside the clearing system's or settlement system's control?

Answer (ok): shall accept delivery at that later date

Clearly asks the bidder’s obligation when the stated delay occurs.

Question (5/5): Are successful bidders or their successors in title entitled to any remedy other than the remedy provided for failure to deliver auctioned allowances caused by circumstances outside the clearing system's or settlement system's control?

Answer (ok): The remedy provided for in paragraph 1 shall be the sole remedy to which a successful bidder or its successors in title shall be entitled to in case of any failure to deliver auctioned allowances, due to circumstances outside the control of the clearing system or settlement system concerned.

A useful question about remedy exclusivity with the triggering circumstances supplied.

original

Both questions are concise and substantive.

Question (5/5): What must the clearing or settlement system do when it cannot deliver auctioned allowances because of circumstances outside its control?

Answer (ok): shall deliver the allowances at the earliest opportunity

Clear question about the system’s required response to the specified failure.

Question (5/5): May a successful bidder or its successor in title claim any remedy other than the remedy specified for late delivery of auctioned allowances caused by circumstances outside the system's control?

Answer (ok): The remedy provided for in paragraph 1 shall be the sole remedy to which a successful bidder or its successors in title shall be entitled to in case of any failure to deliver auctioned allowances, due to circumstances outside the control of the clearing system or settlement system concerned.

The entitlement to additional remedies is a genuine and answerable legal issue.

simple

Good questions; one answer is an unresolved cross-reference instead of the requested remedy.

Question (5/5): Under the rules on late delivery of the auctioned allowances, what must the clearing or settlement system and the successful bidders or their successors in title do when delivery is prevented by circumstances outside the system’s control?

Answer (ok): the clearing system or settlement system shall deliver the allowances at the earliest opportunity and the successful bidders or their successors in title shall accept delivery at that later date.

The system’s and bidder’s linked duties are one coherent delayed-delivery issue.

Question (5/5): Under the rules on late delivery of the auctioned allowances, what remedy is available to a successful bidder or its successor in title when auctioned allowances are not delivered because of circumstances outside the clearing or settlement system’s control?

Answer (major): The remedy provided for in paragraph 1 shall be the sole remedy to which a successful bidder or its successors in title shall be entitled to in case of any failure to deliver auctioned allowances, due to circumstances outside the control of the clearing system or settlement system concerned.

The question clearly asks which remedy exists; the answer states only exclusivity of an unspecified paragraph-1 remedy.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/dir/2006/60/art_2/oj · fact_pattern

The source concerns differentiated national implementation dates and notifications to the Commission. No group generated a question; abstention is not a zero-quality question.

Target source text
1. Member States shall adopt and publish, by 20 January 2007 at the latest, the laws, regulations and administrative provisions necessary to comply with this Directive, except for the benomyl group and thiophanate-methyl for which they shall adopt and publish these by fourteen September 2006 and for chlormequat by thirty one July 2006. They shall forthwith communicate to the Commission the text of those provisions and a correlation table between those provisions and this Directive.They shall apply those provisions from 21 January 2007, except for the benomyl group and thiophanate-methyl for which they shall be applied by fifteen September 2006 and for chlormequat by the first of August 2006.When Member States adopt those provisions, they shall contain a reference to this Directive or be accompanied by such a reference on the occasion of their official publication. Member States shall determine how such reference is to be made.

2. Member States shall communicate to the Commission the text of the main provisions of national law which they adopt in the field covered by this Directive.

Reviewer preference: none. All groups abstained; there is no question-quality comparison.

original

Abstention; no candidate to rate.

revised

Abstention; no candidate to rate.

simple

Abstention; no candidate to rate.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2014/912/art_8/oj · fact_pattern

The Commission must immediately notify the Member State on receipt; a receiving Member State must immediately notify the Commission. Parliament/Council receive specified information within 15 working days of Commission receipt.

Target source text
Notice of intention to initiate arbitration proceedings

1. Where the Commission receives notice by which a claimant states its intention to initiate arbitration proceedings, in accordance with an agreement, it shall immediately notify the Member State concerned. When a claimant states its intention to initiate arbitration proceedings against the Union or a Member State, the Commission shall inform the European Parliament and the Council, within 15 working days of receiving the notice, of the name of the claimant, the provisions of the agreement alleged to have been breached, the economic sector involved, the treatment alleged to be in breach of the agreement and the amount of damages claimed.

2. Where a Member State receives notice by which a claimant states its intention to initiate arbitration proceedings, it shall immediately notify the Commission.

3. The Commission shall inform the European Parliament and the Council of any such notices of intention to initiate arbitration proceedings.

Reviewer preference: simple, original. A and B consistently make the receipt event and notification duties clear. C’s second question leaves the initial recipient implicit.

simple

Both questions clearly identify the receiving authority and requested duty.

Question (5/5): When the Commission receives a claimant’s notice of an intention to initiate arbitration proceedings against the Union or a Member State, what must it report to the European Parliament and the Council, and within what period?

Answer (ok): the Commission shall inform the European Parliament and the Council, within 15 working days of receiving the notice, of the name of the claimant, the provisions of the agreement alleged to have been breached, the economic sector involved, the treatment alleged to be in breach of the agreement and the amount of damages claimed

The information package and its deadline form a coherent, clearly triggered notification question.

Question (5/5): What must a Member State do when a claimant gives it notice of an intention to initiate arbitration proceedings under an agreement?

Answer (ok): it shall immediately notify the Commission

Receipt by a Member State is explicit, making its immediate notification duty answerable.

original

Three clear questions covering distinct notification duties.

Question (5/5): A Member State's investment ministry receives a notice from a foreign claimant stating its intention to initiate arbitration proceedings under an international investment agreement. What must the ministry do?

Answer (ok): immediately notify the Commission

The ministry’s receipt of investment-arbitration notice supplies the trigger for notifying the Commission.

Question (5/5): A Canadian investor gives the Commission a notice stating its intention to initiate arbitration proceedings against a Member State, alleging breach of an investment agreement over telecommunications treatment and claiming damages of 2 million EUR. By when must the European Parliament and the Council be informed?

Answer (ok): within 15 working days of receiving the notice

A concrete investment claim received by the Commission gives a clear question about the reporting deadline.

Question (5/5): A foreign investor sends the Commission a notice stating its intention to initiate arbitration proceedings against a Member State over treatment of its energy investment. What must the Commission do immediately?

Answer (ok): notify the Member State concerned

Commission receipt and the affected Member State make the immediate notification need precise.

revised

One precise question and one with a minor missing procedural link.

Question (5/5): A Member State’s investment ministry receives a notice from a claimant stating its intention to initiate arbitration proceedings against that Member State under an international investment agreement. What must the ministry do?

Answer (ok): it shall immediately notify the Commission

The receiving ministry and arbitration against its Member State give a clear procedural question.

Question (4/5): A claimant in the renewable-energy sector sends notice of an intention to initiate arbitration proceedings against the Union, alleging that treaty provisions were breached and claiming 12 million EUR in damages. What must be communicated to the European Parliament and the Council, and within what time?

Answer (ok): the Commission shall inform the European Parliament and the Council, within 15 working days of receiving the notice, of the name of the claimant, the provisions of the agreement alleged to have been breached, the economic sector involved, the treatment alleged to be in breach of the agreement and the amount of damages claimed

The information need is useful, but the recipient of the claimant’s notice is not stated; the deadline runs from Commission receipt.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2014/912/art_8/oj · lookup

The Commission must immediately notify the Member State on receipt; a receiving Member State must immediately notify the Commission. Parliament/Council receive specified information within 15 working days of Commission receipt.

Target source text
Notice of intention to initiate arbitration proceedings

1. Where the Commission receives notice by which a claimant states its intention to initiate arbitration proceedings, in accordance with an agreement, it shall immediately notify the Member State concerned. When a claimant states its intention to initiate arbitration proceedings against the Union or a Member State, the Commission shall inform the European Parliament and the Council, within 15 working days of receiving the notice, of the name of the claimant, the provisions of the agreement alleged to have been breached, the economic sector involved, the treatment alleged to be in breach of the agreement and the amount of damages claimed.

2. Where a Member State receives notice by which a claimant states its intention to initiate arbitration proceedings, it shall immediately notify the Commission.

3. The Commission shall inform the European Parliament and the Council of any such notices of intention to initiate arbitration proceedings.

Reviewer preference: simple, revised, original. The sets differ in which notification points they cover, without a material question-quality difference.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/dir/2014/59/art_77/oj · fact_pattern

Protection prevents partial transfers and ancillary modification/termination of protected netting/set-off/title-transfer collateral positions. Protection requires entitlement to set off or net. Covered-deposit availability permits a specific exception.

Target source text
Protection for financial collateral, set off and netting agreements

1. Member States shall ensure that there is appropriate protection for title transfer financial collateral arrangements and set-off and netting arrangements so as to prevent the transfer of some, but not all, of the rights and liabilities that are protected under a title transfer financial collateral arrangement, a set-off arrangement or a netting arrangement between the institution under resolution and another person and the modification or termination of rights and liabilities that are protected under such a title transfer financial collateral arrangement, a set-off arrangement or a netting arrangement through the use of ancillary powers.For the purposes of the first subparagraph, rights and liabilities are to be treated as protected under such an arrangement if the parties to the arrangement are entitled to set-off or net those rights and liabilities.

2. Notwithstanding paragraph 1, where necessary in order to ensure availability of the covered deposits the resolution authority may:

(a)
transfer covered deposits which are part of any of the arrangements mentioned in paragraph 1 without transferring other assets, rights or liabilities that are part of the same arrangement; and

(b)
transfer, modify or terminate those assets, rights or liabilities without transferring the covered deposits.

Reviewer preference: revised, original. A and B preserve the decisive title-transfer and deposit-availability distinctions. C includes one materially underspecified collateral case.

revised

Three useful concrete protection/exception questions; legal detail is relevant rather than mere padding.

Question (5/5): A resolution authority handling a credit institution under resolution needs to ensure the availability of covered deposits. The deposits are included in a netting arrangement together with other assets, rights and liabilities, and the authority is considering leaving the deposits in place while changing the treatment of the other items. May it transfer, modify or terminate those other items without transferring the covered deposits?

Answer (ok): transfer, modify or terminate those assets, rights or liabilities without transferring the covered deposits

The necessity to ensure covered-deposit availability and netting arrangement make this exception question answerable.

Question (5/5): A resolution authority handling a credit institution under resolution needs to ensure the availability of its covered deposits. Those deposits form part of a title transfer financial collateral arrangement that also includes other assets and liabilities. May the authority transfer the covered deposits without transferring the other assets and liabilities in that arrangement?

Answer (ok): transfer covered deposits which are part of any of the arrangements mentioned in paragraph 1 without transferring other assets, rights or liabilities that are part of the same arrangement

The covered deposits, title-transfer arrangement and availability need establish the relevant exception.

Question (5/5): A credit institution under resolution has a set-off arrangement with an investment firm, and the parties can net their mutual rights and liabilities. The resolution authority is considering using ancillary powers to transfer only some of those rights and liabilities and to modify or terminate the remainder. What protection must the Member State ensure for the arrangement?

Answer (minor): appropriate protection for title transfer financial collateral arrangements and set-off and netting arrangements so as to prevent the transfer of some, but not all, of the rights and liabilities that are protected under a title transfer financial collateral arrangement, a set-off arrangement or a netting arrangement between the institution under resolution and another person and the modification or termination of rights and liabilities that are protected under such a title transfer financial collateral arrangement, a set-off arrangement or a netting arrangement through the use of ancillary powers

The proposed selective treatment gives a useful protection question; the answer states the general rule without its covered-deposit exception.

original

Both questions are clear and substantive.

Question (5/5): A resolution authority handling a credit institution under resolution finds that covered deposits are part of a title transfer financial collateral arrangement and needs to ensure their availability. May it transfer the covered deposits without also transferring the other assets, rights or liabilities in that arrangement?

Answer (ok): transfer covered deposits which are part of any of the arrangements mentioned in paragraph 1 without transferring other assets, rights or liabilities that are part of the same arrangement

The title-transfer arrangement and need to maintain deposit availability make the permission question well specified.

Question (5/5): A credit institution under resolution has a title transfer financial collateral arrangement with a corporate counterparty, and the parties are entitled to set-off their mutual rights and liabilities. Answer (minor): appropriate protection for title transfer financial collateral arrangements and set-off and netting arrangements so as to prevent the transfer of some, but not all, of the rights and liabilities that are protected under a title transfer financial collateral arrangement, a set-off arrangement or a netting arrangement between the institution under resolution and another person and the modification or termination of rights and liabilities that are protected under such a title transfer financial collateral arrangement, a set-off arrangement or a netting arrangement through the use of ancillary powers

Entitlement to set off and proposed partial transfer create a clear protection question; the answer omits the possible deposit-availability exception.

simple

Two strong questions, but the collateral scenario omits a decisive subtype.

Question (5/5): A national authority is handling the resolution of a credit institution that has title transfer financial collateral and set-off arrangements with another person. What protection must Member States ensure against selective transfers or changes made through ancillary powers?

Answer (minor): Member States shall ensure that there is appropriate protection for title transfer financial collateral arrangements and set-off and netting arrangements so as to prevent the transfer of some, but not all, of the rights and liabilities that are protected under a title transfer financial collateral arrangement, a set-off arrangement or a netting arrangement between the institution under resolution and another person and the modification or termination of rights and liabilities that are protected under such a title transfer financial collateral arrangement, a set-off arrangement or a netting arrangement through the use of ancillary powers.

A clear question about resolution protections; the answer would be more complete with the protected-rights condition and deposit exception.

Question (3/5): A resolution authority is handling covered deposits that form part of a financial collateral arrangement, and separating them is necessary to ensure their availability. May the authority transfer those deposits without transferring the other assets, rights or liabilities in the same arrangement?

Answer (major): where necessary in order to ensure availability of the covered deposits the resolution authority may: (a) transfer covered deposits which are part of any of the arrangements mentioned in paragraph 1 without transferring other assets, rights or liabilities that are part of the same arrangement;

Financial collateral is broader than title-transfer financial collateral; the missing arrangement type can change whether this exception applies.

Question (5/5): In a resolution file, an institution and another person are parties to a netting arrangement and are entitled to net the arrangement's rights and liabilities. Which rights and liabilities are treated as protected under the arrangement?

Answer (ok): rights and liabilities are to be treated as protected under such an arrangement if the parties to the arrangement are entitled to set-off or net those rights and liabilities.

The netting entitlement is a case fact; whether those rights receive legal protection remains a useful unknown.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2014/312/art_16/oj · fact_pattern

Coexisting daily/hourly gas nominations permit consultation on harmonisation. Consultation must consider three impacts; regulators approve changes and operators amend agreements/contracts and publish them.

Target source text
Specific provisions at interconnection points

1. Where daily and hourly nominations and re-nominations co-exist at an interconnection point, the transmission system operators or national regulatory authorities (as appropriate) may consult the stakeholders for the purpose of identifying whether harmonised nominations and re-nominations should be submitted at both sides of this interconnection point. This consultation shall consider at least the following:

(a)
financial impact on transmission system operators and network users;

(b)
impact on cross-border trade;

(c)
impact on the daily balancing regime at the interconnection point(s).

2. Following this consultation, the proposed changes, if any, shall be approved by the national regulatory authorities. Once the proposed changes are approved the transmission system operators shall amend accordingly the existing interconnection agreements and the transport contracts or other legally binding agreements and publish those changes.

Reviewer preference: revised, original, simple. All groups express substantive, clearly scoped questions; different amounts of scenario framing do not distinguish quality.

revised

Both questions give enough context and ask useful procedural points.

Question (5/5): A gas transmission system operator at an interconnection point has daily and hourly nominations and re-nominations in use and consults stakeholders about harmonising submissions on both sides. Which impacts must the consultation consider?

Answer (ok): financial impact on transmission system operators and network users; impact on cross-border trade; impact on the daily balancing regime at the interconnection point(s).

The gas-interconnection consultation and coexistence of nominations identify a clear request for required impact considerations.

Question (5/5): Two transmission system operators have an interconnection agreement and transport contracts in place, and proposed changes to harmonise nominations at their interconnection point have been approved by the national regulatory authorities after consultation. What must the operators do?

Answer (ok): the transmission system operators shall amend accordingly the existing interconnection agreements and the transport contracts or other legally binding agreements and publish those changes.

Approval and existing agreements create a clear operational question about implementing changes.

Answer (ok): may consult the stakeholders for the purpose of identifying whether harmonised nominations and re-nominations should be submitted at both sides of this interconnection point

The proposed consultation is a real permission question; stating its intended purpose does not reveal whether consultation is permitted.

Question (5/5): A national regulatory authority is consulting stakeholders about possible harmonised nominations at a gas transmission interconnection point where daily and hourly nominations and re-nominations coexist. Which impacts must the consultation consider?

Answer (ok): financial impact on transmission system operators and network users; impact on cross-border trade; impact on the daily balancing regime at the interconnection point(s)

Clearly asks the required considerations in the specified gas consultation.

Transposition

1. Member States shall adopt and publish, by 30 June 2006 at the latest, the laws, regulations and administrative provisions necessary to comply with this Directive. eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2014/165/art_23/oj · fact_pattern

Regular tachograph inspections occur at least every two years and cover seven listed checks. Remedied irregularities require a report, retained at least two years from creation and made available when retained by workshops and requested.

Target source text
Inspections of tachographs

1. Tachographs shall be subject to regular inspection by approved workshops. Regular inspections shall be carried out at least every two years.

2. The inspections referred to in paragraph 1 shall check at least the following:

the tachograph is correctly fitted and appropriate for the vehicle;

the tachograph is working properly;

the tachograph carries the type-approval mark;

the installation plaque is affixed;

all seals are intact and effective;

there are no manipulation devices attached to the tachograph or traces of the use of such devices;

the tyre size and the actual circumference of the tyres.

3. Workshops shall draw up an inspection report in cases where irregularities in the functioning of the tachograph had to be remedied, whether as a result of a periodic inspection or of an inspection carried out at the specific request of the national competent authority. They shall keep a list of all inspection reports drawn up.

4. Inspection reports shall be retained for a minimum period of two years from the time the report was made. Member States shall decide whether inspection reports are to be retained or sent to the competent authority during that period. In cases where the inspection reports are kept by the workshop, upon request from the competent authority, the workshop shall make available the reports of inspections and calibrations carried out during that period.

Reviewer preference: revised, original. A and B consistently ask useful, well-identified questions; A’s answer incompleteness does not reduce those question ratings. C contains a materially unanchored question.

revised

Strong questions; two answers need fuller scope or an explicit period.

Question (5/5): An approved workshop is carrying out a regular inspection of a tachograph fitted to a haulage vehicle. It has checked the installation and operation but has not examined the vehicle's seals or possible manipulation equipment. What must the inspection also check?

Answer (major): all seals are intact and effective; there are no manipulation devices attached to the tachograph or traces of the use of such devices

The question usefully asks what remains to inspect; the answer lists seals and manipulation but omits other unchecked items such as the plaque, approval mark and tyres.

Question (5/5): A road-haulage operator uses a vehicle fitted with a tachograph, and an approved workshop has completed its regular inspection. How often must the tachograph undergo another regular inspection?

Answer (ok): at least every two years

The vehicle and approved inspection establish a clear, useful repeat-inspection interval question.

Question (5/5): An approved workshop remedied irregular functioning found during a tachograph inspection and prepared an inspection report. The Member State has decided that workshops retain such reports, and the national competent authority now requests the records. What must the workshop make available?

Answer (major): the reports of inspections and calibrations carried out during that period

The workshop-retention decision and request make the disclosure question clear; the answer leaves during that period undefined.

original

Three clear operational questions with sufficient premises.

Question (5/5): An approved tachograph workshop finds and remedies an irregularity during an inspection specifically requested by the national competent authority. What must the workshop prepare?

Answer (ok): shall draw up an inspection report

The requested inspection and remedied irregularity identify the report-making duty exactly.

Question (5/5): A tachograph workshop has kept reports of inspections and calibrations carried out 18 months ago, and the competent authority has requested them. What must the workshop do?

Answer (ok): upon request from the competent authority, the workshop shall make available the reports of inspections and calibrations carried out during that period

An authority request for retained recent records creates a clear disclosure obligation.

Question (5/5): A haulage company operates a vehicle fitted with a tachograph, and an approved workshop carried out its last regular inspection 20 months ago. How often must the tachograph undergo a regular inspection?

Answer (ok): at least every two years

The concrete last inspection and vehicle establish a useful regular-inspection frequency question.

simple

Two strong questions and one generically framed records question.

Question (5/5): For a tachograph installed in a road-transport vehicle, how often must an approved workshop carry out a regular inspection?

Answer (ok): at least every two years

Clear frequency question with the tachograph and approved workshop specified.

Question (3/5): When a national competent authority requests reports from a workshop that has retained them, which reports must the workshop make available?

Answer (major): the reports of inspections and calibrations carried out during that period.

Neither tachographs nor road transport identifies the workshop reports; the answer also leaves the relevant period unspecified.

Question (5/5): During an inspection of a road-transport vehicle's tachograph, what must be checked concerning manipulation devices?

Answer (ok): there are no manipulation devices attached to the tachograph or traces of the use of such devices;

The manipulation-device check is a precise substantive safety requirement.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2014/165/art_23/oj · lookup

Regular tachograph inspections occur at least every two years and cover seven listed checks. Remedied irregularities require a report, retained at least two years from creation and made available when retained by workshops and requested.

Target source text
Inspections of tachographs

1. Tachographs shall be subject to regular inspection by approved workshops. Regular inspections shall be carried out at least every two years.

2. The inspections referred to in paragraph 1 shall check at least the following:

the tachograph is correctly fitted and appropriate for the vehicle;

the tachograph is working properly;

the tachograph carries the type-approval mark;

the installation plaque is affixed;

all seals are intact and effective;

there are no manipulation devices attached to the tachograph or traces of the use of such devices;

the tyre size and the actual circumference of the tyres.

3. Workshops shall draw up an inspection report in cases where irregularities in the functioning of the tachograph had to be remedied, whether as a result of a periodic inspection or of an inspection carried out at the specific request of the national competent authority. They shall keep a list of all inspection reports drawn up.

4. Inspection reports shall be retained for a minimum period of two years from the time the report was made. Member States shall decide whether inspection reports are to be retained or sent to the competent authority during that period. In cases where the inspection reports are kept by the workshop, upon request from the competent authority, the workshop shall make available the reports of inspections and calibrations carried out during that period.

Reviewer preference: revised, original. A and C sustain natural, precise questions; B has minor repetitive scaffolding rather than substantive errors.

revised

All three are clear substantive questions.

Question (5/5): For how long must inspection reports for tachographs in road transport be retained?

Answer (ok): for a minimum period of two years from the time the report was made

Clear retention question with the tachograph-report context supplied.

Question (5/5): When must workshops draw up an inspection report for tachographs in road transport?

Answer (ok): Workshops shall draw up an inspection report in cases where irregularities in the functioning of the tachograph had to be remedied, whether as a result of a periodic inspection or of an inspection carried out at the specific request of the national competent authority.

A useful question about the circumstances requiring a report, fully resolved by the source.

Question (5/5): How often must tachographs in road transport undergo regular inspection by approved workshops?

Answer (ok): at least every two years

Natural and precise question about the inspection interval.

simple

Useful questions with some avoidable template phrasing.

Question (4/5): For inspections of tachographs, how often must tachographs be subject to regular inspection by approved workshops?

Answer (ok): Regular inspections shall be carried out at least every two years.

The inspection-frequency need is clear, but the opening repeats inspections of tachographs unnecessarily.

Question (4/5): For inspections of tachographs, for how long must inspection reports be retained?

Answer (ok): Inspection reports shall be retained for a minimum period of two years from the time the report was made.

A useful retention question, expressed through a somewhat clumsy repeated for construction.

Question (5/5): For inspections of tachographs, when must workshops draw up an inspection report?

Answer (ok): Workshops shall draw up an inspection report in cases where irregularities in the functioning of the tachograph had to be remedied, whether as a result of a periodic inspection or of an inspection carried out at the specific request of the national competent authority.

Question (5/5): How often must tachographs in road transport undergo regular inspections by approved workshops?

Answer (ok): at least every two years

Clearly identifies the regulated device, inspection and responsible workshops.

Question (5/5): When must approved workshops draw up an inspection report for a tachograph?

Answer (ok): in cases where irregularities in the functioning of the tachograph had to be remedied, whether as a result of a periodic inspection or of an inspection carried out at the specific request of the national competent authority

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2013/1381/art_5/oj · fact_pattern

The Rights, Equality and Citizenship Programme finances analytical, training, cooperation/dissemination and main-actor support activities. Beneficiaries must encourage relevant target-group participation. Listing a category does not guarantee individual funding.

Target source text
Types of actions

1. The Programme shall finance inter alia the following types of actions:

(a)
analytical activities, such as the collection of data and statistics; the development of common methodologies and, where appropriate, indicators or benchmarks; studies, researches, analyses and surveys; evaluations; the elaboration and publication of guides, reports and educational material; workshops, seminars, experts' meetings and conferences;

(b)
training activities, such as staff exchanges, workshops, seminars, train-the-trainer events and the development of online training tools or other training modules;

(c)
mutual learning, cooperation, awareness-raising and dissemination activities, such as the identification of, and exchanges concerning, good practices, innovative approaches and experiences; the organisation of peer reviews and mutual learning; the organisation of conferences, seminars, media campaigns, including in the online media, information campaigns, including institutional communication on the political priorities of the Union as far as they relate to the objectives of the Programme; the compilation and publication of materials to disseminate information about the Programme and its results; the development, operation and maintenance of systems and tools using information and communication technologies;

(d)
support for main actors whose activities contribute to the implementation of the objectives of the Programme, such as support for NGOs in the implementation of actions with European added value, support for key European actors, European-level networks and harmonised services of social value; support for Member States in the implementation of Union law and policies; and support for networking activities at European level among specialised bodies and entities as well as national, regional and local authorities and NGOs, including support by way of action grants or operating grants.

2. In order to ensure an inclusive perspective, beneficiaries shall encourage the participation of relevant target groups in actions financed by the Programme.

Reviewer preference: simple. C supplies the programme context consistently and has two strong questions; A and B each contain more material context or circularity weaknesses.

original

One well-scoped question and two that omit the governing programme.

Question (3/5): An NGO receiving programme funding wants to run an online training course for staff working on equality projects. What type of action does this fall under?

Answer (ok): training activities, such as staff exchanges, workshops, seminars, train-the-trainer events and the development of online training tools or other training modules

The training-classification need is answerable in principle, but programme funding and equality projects do not identify which programme’s categories apply.

Question (3/5): A beneficiary is organising a conference for young people affected by discrimination as part of a funded awareness campaign. What must it do to ensure the action has an inclusive perspective?

Answer (ok): beneficiaries shall encourage the participation of relevant target groups in actions financed by the Programme

The inclusion duty is a useful need, but the question never identifies the funding programme whose duty it invokes.

Question (5/5): A civil-rights research institute applying for support under a Union programme on rights, equality and citizenship plans to collect data and statistics on discrimination. Is this a type of action that the programme may finance?

Answer (ok): analytical activities, such as the collection of data and statistics

The named policy programme and data-collection proposal make a clear permitted-action-category question.

revised

One strong question, one missing programme context and one circular classification attempt.

Question (5/5): An NGO applying for funding under an EU rights, equality and citizenship programme proposes to collect data and statistics and develop a common methodology on access to justice. What type of action does this proposal represent?

Answer (ok): analytical activities, such as the collection of data and statistics; the development of common methodologies

The rights/equality/citizenship programme and concrete analytical proposal make category identification a real, answerable need.

Question (3/5): A beneficiary is organising an awareness campaign on equal treatment, but relevant target groups have not yet been involved in planning the campaign. What must the beneficiary do in relation to those groups?

Answer (ok): beneficiaries shall encourage the participation of relevant target groups in actions financed by the Programme

The participation issue is clear, but beneficiary and awareness campaign lack the programme context needed to select the rule.

Question (3/5): A European-level NGO network is applying for operating support because its activities contribute to the objectives of an EU rights, equality and citizenship programme and its proposed work has European added value. What type of action can the funding support?

Answer (major): support for NGOs in the implementation of actions with European added value

The question already describes NGO operating support with European added value; the answer restates that activity rather than identifying a distinct action category.

simple

Two strong programme-specific questions and one circular support question.

Question (5/5): What type of action under the Rights, Equality and Citizenship Programme covers a project collecting data and statistics?

Answer (ok): analytical activities, such as the collection of data and statistics

The programme is identified and the analytical category is a genuine unknown.

Question (3/5): What type of support under the Rights, Equality and Citizenship Programme concerns an NGO implementing an action with European added value?

Answer (major): support for NGOs in the implementation of actions with European added value

The NGO action with European added value is already specified, and the supplied support answer merely repeats it rather than providing a new category.

Question (5/5): What must beneficiaries do to ensure an inclusive perspective in actions financed by the Rights, Equality and Citizenship Programme?

Answer (ok): beneficiaries shall encourage the participation of relevant target groups in actions financed by the Programme

Clearly asks a substantive beneficiary obligation in the named programme.

The identified German-intervention rye tender for the Spanish market has first, subsequent and final invitation deadlines, excluded weeks, and a named lodging agency. A trader’s first bid is not necessarily the first partial invitation.

D-53179 Bonn

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2014/788/art_20/oj · fact_pattern

The Commission informs the recognised maritime organisation about remedies, notifies the Maritime Safety Agency and Member States for information, and may publish justified decisions while respecting legitimate interests.

Target source text
Judicial remedies, notification and publication

1. The Commission shall inform the recognised organisation concerned of the judicial remedies available to it.

2. The Commission shall notify its decision to the European Maritime Safety Agency and to the Member States for information.

3. When justified, in particular on grounds of safety or protection of the environment, the Commission may make its decision public. When publishing details of its decision or informing the Member States, the Commission shall have regard to the legitimate interests of the recognised organisation concerned and other interested persons.

Reviewer preference: simple. A consistently identifies the maritime organisation; B and C leave that material retrieval context unstated.

simple

Both questions need the maritime regime stated to become reliable standalone queries.

Answer (ok): the judicial remedies available to it

A challenge to a fine is a useful need, but recognised organisation does not identify the maritime sector.

Question (3/5): A Commission decision concerning a recognised organisation addresses a serious safety issue, while publication could affect the organisation's legitimate interests. What limits apply if the Commission considers making the decision public?

Answer (ok): When justified, in particular on grounds of safety or protection of the environment, the Commission may make its decision public. When publishing details of its decision or informing the Member States, the Commission shall have regard to the legitimate interests of the recognised organisation concerned and other interested persons.

Publication limits are useful, but the question omits what kind of recognised organisation and Commission proceeding is involved.

The publication-interests need is clear, but the recognised organisation’s sector is unstated.

Answer (ok): the European Maritime Safety Agency and the Member States for information

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2014/788/art_20/oj · lookup

The Commission informs the recognised maritime organisation about remedies, notifies the Maritime Safety Agency and Member States for information, and may publish justified decisions while respecting legitimate interests.

Target source text
Judicial remedies, notification and publication

1. The Commission shall inform the recognised organisation concerned of the judicial remedies available to it.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2006/1906/art_50/oj · fact_pattern

Access for using foreground requires need; background also requires entitlement to grant. Terms may be fair/reasonable or royalty-free. Affiliate rights can be overridden by agreement; the default request period is one year unless participants agree otherwise.

Target source text
Access rights for use

1. Participants in the same indirect action shall enjoy access rights to foreground, if it is needed to use their own foreground.Subject to agreement, such access rights shall be granted either under fair and reasonable conditions or be royalty-free.

2. Participants in the same indirect action shall enjoy access rights to background, if it is needed to use their own foreground provided that the participant concerned is entitled to grant them.Subject to agreement, such access rights shall be granted either under fair and reasonable conditions or be royalty-free.

3. An affiliated entity established in a Member State or associated country shall also have access rights, referred to in paragraphs 1 and 2, to foreground or background under the same conditions as the participant to which it is affiliated, unless otherwise provided for in the grant agreement or consortium agreement.

4. A request for access rights under paragraphs 1, 2 and 3 may be made up to one year after either of the following events:

(a)
the end of the indirect action;

(b)
termination of participation by the owner of the background or foreground concerned.

However, the participants concerned may agree on a different time-limit.
5. Subject to the agreement of all the owners concerned, access rights to foreground shall be granted to a RTD performer, on fair and reasonable conditions to be agreed, for the purposes of pursuing further research activities.

6. RTD performers shall grant access on a royalty-free basis, or on fair and reasonable conditions to be agreed prior to the signing of the grant agreement, to background needed to use the foreground generated in the indirect action.

Reviewer preference: revised, simple. A and B consistently ask clear substantive questions; their answer completeness differs, but that is scored separately. C has a minor question-context weakness.

revised

Three strong questions; the first answer omits a material deadline qualification.

Question (5/5): A participant in an indirect action intends to request access to foreground 10 months after the action has ended. Until when may the request be made?

Answer (major): up to one year after the end of the indirect action

The access-request deadline is a useful question; the answer presents one year absolutely and omits the permitted agreed alternative.

Question (5/5): A university and a research centre are participants in the same indirect action, and the university needs the research centre's foreground to use its own foreground. On what terms must the access be granted?

Answer (ok): Subject to agreement, such access rights shall be granted either under fair and reasonable conditions or be royalty-free.

The relationship and need for another participant’s foreground make the access-terms question clear.

Question (5/5): An RTD performer generated foreground in an indirect action, and another participant needs access to its background to use that foreground. On what terms must the RTD performer grant access?

Answer (ok): RTD performers shall grant access on a royalty-free basis, or on fair and reasonable conditions to be agreed prior to the signing of the grant agreement, to background needed to use the foreground generated in the indirect action.

The RTD performer’s background and its use for generated foreground identify the specific terms obligation.

Three clearly scoped direct questions; absence of invented scenario detail is not a quality defect.

Question (5/5): When may a participant in the same indirect action obtain access to another participant's foreground for using its own foreground, and on what terms?

Answer (ok): Participants in the same indirect action shall enjoy access rights to foreground, if it is needed to use their own foreground.Subject to agreement, such access rights shall be granted either under fair and reasonable conditions or be royalty-free.

Eligibility and terms form one coherent access-rights need in a shared research action.

Question (5/5): What access rights does an affiliated entity established in a Member State have to foreground or background when it is affiliated with a participant in the same indirect action?

Answer (minor): An affiliated entity established in a Member State or associated country shall also have access rights, referred to in paragraphs 1 and 2, to foreground or background under the same conditions as the participant to which it is affiliated, unless otherwise provided for in the grant agreement or consortium agreement.

The affiliate’s location and relationship identify a real rights question; the answer refers to participant conditions without explaining the underlying need and grant-entitlement tests.

Question (5/5): What is the latest default period for requesting access rights after an indirect action ends or the owner of the relevant background or foreground terminates its participation?

Answer (ok): A request for access rights under paragraphs 1, 2 and 3 may be made up to one year after either of the following events: (a) the end of the indirect action; (b) termination of participation by the owner of the background or foreground concerned. 4. However, the participants concerned may agree on a different time-limit.

Explicitly requests the default period and the source states both triggering events and the contractual exception.
