eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2012/966/art_56/oj · fact_pattern

Institutions may internally delegate budget powers within regulatory, internal-rule and delegation-instrument limits. Heads of Union Delegations may receive operational-appropriation powers, subject to Commission rules and equivalent accountability; the High Representative is informed.

Target source text
Delegation of budget implementation powers

1. The Commission and each of the other institutions may, within their departments, delegate their powers of budget implementation in accordance with the conditions laid down in this Regulation and by their internal rules and within the limits which they lay down in the instrument of delegation. Those so empowered shall act within the limits of the powers expressly conferred upon them.

2. However, the Commission may delegate its powers of budget implementation concerning the operational appropriations of its own section to the Heads of Union Delegations. It shall, at the same time, inform the High Representative thereof. When Heads of Union Delegations act as subdelegated authorising officers of the Commission, they shall apply the Commission rules for the implementation of the budget and shall be subject to the same duties, obligations and accountability as any other subdelegated authorising officer of the Commission.The Commission may withdraw the delegation in accordance with its own rules.For the purposes of the first subparagraph, the High Representative shall take the measures necessary to facilitate cooperation between Union Delegations and Commission departments.

Reviewer preference: simple. C supplies clear substantive questions while the other groups abstain. This is coverage plus question usability, not proof of stronger hypothetical scenarios.

revised

Abstention; no candidate to rate.

Abstention; no candidate to rate.

Clearly asks the framework limiting internal delegation; the answer identifies the governing instruments but leaves their detailed conditions unresolved.

Question (5/5): What rules, duties, obligations and accountability apply when a Head of a Union Delegation acts as a subdelegated authorising officer of the Commission for operational appropriations?

Answer (ok): When Heads of Union Delegations act as subdelegated authorising officers of the Commission, they shall apply the Commission rules for the implementation of the budget and shall be subject to the same duties, obligations and accountability as any other subdelegated authorising officer of the Commission.

A clear institutional question about the rules and accountability attached to a specified role.

Question (5/5): When the Commission delegates budget implementation powers concerning operational appropriations of its own section to Heads of Union Delegations, whom must it inform at the same time?

The delegation and simultaneous notification duty are clearly identified.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg/2009/436/art_36/oj · fact_pattern

Professional/commercial holders of wine products must keep entry/withdrawal registers. Stockless traders may be required by Member States to do so. Each annotation needs supporting documentation; supplied Article 41 identifies bottling and other operations plus required details.

Target source text
Purpose

1. Natural or legal persons and groups of persons who hold wine products, for whatever professional or commercial purposes, shall be required to keep entry and withdrawal registers for those products, hereinafter called ‘registers’.

2. Member States may require traders who do not hold stocks to keep registers in accordance with the rules and procedures they shall specify.

3. Persons required to keep registers shall record the entry and withdrawal of each batch of products as referred to in paragraph 1 to or from their premises, and such of the operations specified in Article 41(1) as are carried out on their premises. Those persons must also be able to present, for each annotation in the entry and withdrawal registers of products, a document which has accompanied the relevant consignment or any other supporting document, in particular a commercial document.

Reviewer preference: revised, original. A and C consistently identify the wine regime. B has one materially unanchored question; A’s incomplete answer is assessed separately.

The commercial wine holder presents a clear record-keeping obligation question.

A specific wine-register annotation creates a useful request for supporting documentation.

Answer (ok): Natural or legal persons and groups of persons who hold wine products, for whatever professional or commercial purposes, shall be required to keep entry and withdrawal registers for those products, hereinafter called ‘registers’.

Clear request for the holder categories and purposes triggering wine-register duties.

Question (5/5): If Member States require traders who do not hold stocks to keep registers for wine products, whose rules and procedures govern those registers?

Answer (ok): in accordance with the rules and procedures they shall specify

Whether national or other rules govern mandated stockless-trader registers is a useful unknown.

Question (5/5): What must holders of wine products who keep entry and withdrawal registers be able to present for each register annotation?

Answer (ok): a document which has accompanied the relevant consignment or any other supporting document, in particular a commercial document

The wine-register annotation and supporting-material need are clearly identified.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/dir/2006/112/art_111/oj · fact_pattern

Article 111 lists Finnish and Swedish supply categories and incorporates Article 110 paragraph two’s EU-law, social-reasons and final-consumer conditions. Category identification does not require proving every condition for actual exemption.

Target source text
Subject to the conditions laid down in the second paragraph of Article 110, exemptions with deductibility of the VAT paid at the preceding stage may continue to be granted in the following cases:

(a)
by Finland in respect of the supply of newspapers and periodicals sold by subscription and the printing of publications distributed to the members of corporations for the public good;

(b)
by Sweden in respect of the supply of newspapers, including radio and cassette newspapers for the visually impaired, pharmaceutical products supplied to hospitals or on prescription, and the production of, or other related services concerning, periodicals of non-profit-making organisations.

Reviewer preference: original, revised. A and B consistently pose clear category applications. C remains good but its conditions question uses a document-dependent reference; direct versus scenario form is not scored.

A Finnish subscription-newspaper supply asks only whether it belongs to a listed category; the category span resolves that genuine legal unknown.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/reg_impl/2014/181/art_5/oj · fact_pattern

Entry into the smaller Aegean islands supply register requires specific undertakings. Export/dispatch intentions and plant locations are declared at application or later. Supplied Articles 11/12 delimit the relevant export/dispatch arrangements.

Target source text
Register of operators

1. In order to be eligible for entry in the register referred to in the second subparagraph of Article 11(1) of Regulation (EU) No 229/2013, operators shall undertake to:

(a)
communicate to the competent authorities, at their request, all relevant information about their commercial activities, particularly regarding the prices and profit margins they practise;

(b)
operate exclusively in their own name and on their own account;

(c)
submit certificate applications commensurate with their real capacity to dispose of the products concerned, such capacity being proven by reference to objective factors;

(d)
refrain from acting in any way likely to create artificial shortages of products and from marketing the available products at artificially low prices;

(e)
ensure to the satisfaction of the competent authorities that, when the agricultural products are disposed of in the smaller islands, the advantage is passed on to the end-user.

2. Operators intending to dispatch to the rest of the Union or export to third countries unprocessed, processed or packaged products under the conditions referred to in Article 11 shall, at the time of applying for entry in the register or later, declare their intention to engage in this activity and indicate the location of the packaging plant where applicable.

3. Processors intending to export to third countries or dispatch to the rest of the Union processed products under the conditions referred to in Article 11 or 12 shall, at the time of applying for entry in the register or later, declare their intention to engage in this activity, indicate the location of the processing plant and, where applicable, provide analytical lists of the processed products.

Reviewer preference: revised, original. A and B have comparably mixed sets but fewer substantial standalone-context failures than C; neither has a clear overall advantage over the other.

revised

One strong capacity question, a slightly repetitive information question and an unanchored export-registration question.

Question (4/5): An operator seeking entry in the register for the smaller Aegean islands supply arrangements has commercial activities for which the competent authorities have requested information about its prices and profit margins. What information must the operator provide?

Answer (ok): all relevant information about their commercial activities, particularly regarding the prices and profit margins they practise

The regime is clear and all relevant commercial information is the answer, but naming the requested prices/margins makes the information need somewhat repetitive.

Question (5/5): An operator applying for certificates under the smaller Aegean islands supply arrangements has warehouse and distribution records showing its actual capacity to dispose of the products concerned. How must the operator determine the scale of its certificate applications?

Answer (ok): submit certificate applications commensurate with their real capacity to dispose of the products concerned, such capacity being proven by reference to objective factors

The certificate application scale and objective capacity evidence give a concrete, useful registration-condition question.

Question (3/5): A processor applying for entry in the register intends to export processed products to third countries and dispatch other processed products to the rest of the Union, with the products made at a specified processing plant. What must the processor do at the time of applying or later?

Answer (ok): declare their intention to engage in this activity, indicate the location of the processing plant and, where applicable, provide analytical lists of the processed products

The processor/export scenario omits the smaller Aegean islands or specific supply arrangements, leaving the register and applicable obligations unidentified.

eurlex · http://data.europa.eu/eli/dir/2014/26/art_2/oj · fact_pattern

The scope rule distinguishes all EU collective management organisations, multi-territorial online musical-work management, controlled entities performing covered activities, and independent management entities. Supplied references clarify the numbered provisions; scope questions can legitimately request those provisions.

Target source text
Scope

1. Titles I, II, IV and V with the exception of Article 34(2) and Article 38 apply to all collective management organisations established in the Union.

2. Title III and Article 34(2) and Article 38 apply to collective management organisations established in the Union managing authors’ rights in musical works for online use on a multi-territorial basis.

3. The relevant provisions of this Directive apply to entities directly or indirectly owned or controlled, wholly or in part, by a collective management organisation, provided that such entities carry out an activity which, if carried out by the collective management organisation, would be subject to the provisions of this Directive.

4. Article 16(1), Articles 18 and 20, points (a), (b), (c), (e), (f) and (g) of Article 21(1) and Articles 36 and 42 apply to all independent management entities established in the Union.

Reviewer preference: revised, original. A and B consistently identify the regulated activities and scope distinctions. C’s weakness is missing framework context, not its direct-question form.

revised

Three strong scope questions with minor citation-only answer limitations.

Question (5/5): A collective management organisation established in France manages authors’ rights in musical works for online use on a multi-territorial basis. Which parts of the EU framework apply specifically to that activity?

Answer (minor): Title III and Article 34(2) and Article 38

The EU establishment and multi-territorial online musical-rights activity clearly identify the special scope question; the answer gives bare provision numbers.

Question (5/5): A company is partly owned by a collective management organisation established in the Union and carries out licensing administration that would be subject to the EU copyright-management rules if performed by that organisation itself. Do the relevant provisions apply to the company?

Answer (ok): The relevant provisions of this Directive apply to entities directly or indirectly owned or controlled, wholly or in part, by a collective management organisation, provided that such entities carry out an activity which, if carried out by the collective management organisation, would be subject to the provisions of this Directive.

The ownership and covered-activity facts make extension to the company a real legal unknown, not leaked answer content.

Question (5/5): An independent management entity established in Belgium administers copyright and related rights for rightsholders. Which transparency, licensing and enforcement provisions apply to it?

Answer (minor): Article 16(1), Articles 18 and 20, points (a), (b), (c), (e), (f) and (g) of Article 21(1) and Articles 36 and 42

The independent entity, EU establishment and copyright work give a clear scope question; the answer needs the instrument identified alongside its numbers.

The independent music-licensing entity and EU location make the applicable-provisions question clear; the answer supplies numbers without an instrument name.

The online author-rights activity across Member States identifies the special regime clearly; the answer is a bare list of provisions.

Question (2/5): At the 15 April 2004 Security Council debate, what role and institutional approach did speakers identify for business in post-conflict stabilization?

Answer (major): There was a general view among speakers that business has an important role to play in conflict prevention and conflict resolution. Private business could fuel conflicts just as it could help to overcome conflicts. Business' role in providing employment, particularly in countries emerging from conflict, was emphasized. The Stability Pact for South Eastern Europe was referred to by some speakers as a good example for a comprehensive approach towards economic and political stabilization. The Kimberly Process for Certification of Rough Diamonds was frequently cited as a good example of partnership with the private sector to reduce the role of trade in diamonds to fund conflicts. The meeting signalled the need felt for a more coherent approach by all respective institutions both inside and outside the United Nations to better use the potential of entrepreneurial initiative in stabilization efforts after a conflict.

Clear substantive question, but business roles and institutional approach are answered only in surrounding context, not this target block; target mismatch, not hallucination.

un · 1998/gc_7/c_1/sr_5#16 · lookup

Target records UNIDO Personnel Services Director Lässig explaining tax-reimbursement authority, equality of employment conditions, and amendment procedure with acquired-rights protection. Context separately supplies Legal Service’s nationality clarification and the reliance-based explanation of acquired rights; those are not target answer substance. Exact meeting date is not explicitly established in the supplied header; adjacent discussion treats May 1998 as forthcoming, so date precision is uncertain.

Target source text
Mr. KOCH (Germany) asked whether the second additional paragraph proposed by the representative of the Russian Federation was in conformity with UNIDO Staff Regulations.
Mr. LÄSSIG (Director, Personnel Services) said that Staff Regulation 6.8 was quite clear: it authorized the Director-General to refund to staff members an amount representing the tax paid on salary and emoluments.
Reimbursement was made by all United Nations agencies on the principle of equality of conditions of employment and irrespective of nationality. Suspension of payment would require the amendment of Staff Regulation 6.8.
The conditions of employment of staff were the same, irrespective of their nationality.
Where a staff member had to pay tax, despite the Convention on the Privileges and Immunities of the Specialized Agencies, the agency concerned sought to restore equality among all staff members through the mechanism of tax reimbursement.
The Staff Regulations were laid down by the Member States by virtue of being adopted by the General Conference.
Staff Regulation 13.1 specified that the Staff Regulations might “be amended by the General Conference upon recommendation of the Industrial Development Board, without prejudice to the acquired rights of the staff”.
So if the Staff Regulations were to be amended, such amendment had to be recommended by the Board and must not prejudice the acquired rights of staff members.

Reviewer preference: simple, revised. Both sets consistently ask useful questions answerable from the target; B includes context-only substance.

Answer (ok): Staff Regulation 13.1 specified that the Staff Regulations might “be amended by the General Conference upon recommendation of the Industrial Development Board, without prejudice to the acquired rights of the staff”. So if the Staff Regulations were to be amended, such amendment had to be recommended by the Board and must not prejudice the acquired rights of staff members.

Clear attributed procedural question covering both recommendation and staff-rights safeguard.

Question (5/5): According to the UNIDO Director of Personnel Services’ 1997 statement on tax reimbursement in a General Conference Committee summary record, what would suspending payment require?

Answer (ok): Suspension of payment would require the amendment of Staff Regulation 6.8.

Concrete suspension requirement, with institution, speaker and dispute clearly identified.

Question (5/5): According to the UNIDO Director of Personnel Services’ 1997 statement on tax reimbursement in a General Conference Committee summary record, on what basis was reimbursement made regardless of nationality?

revised

Question (2/5): Why might suspending tax reimbursement prejudice staff members’ acquired rights?

Answer (major): If staff members for whom a tax reimbursement entitlement was to be suspended had joined the Organization on the understanding that such reimbursement by the Organization was an essential condition of their employment if they were assessed for national tax, they had, presumably, come to rely upon that Staff Regulation as an essential element of their employment, and its amendment would undoubtedly prejudice their acquired rights.

The reliance-based explanation of prejudice appears only in surrounding context; target states the safeguard without explaining why suspension would prejudice rights.

Question (5/5): What did Argentina say it would do during informal consultations regarding UNOTIL's resources?

Answer (ok): His delegation associated itself fully with the statement made by the representative of Brazil and would work constructively during informal consultations to ensure that UNOTIL had all the resources needed to implement its mandate effectively.

Identifies actor, specific mission and consultation issue; sufficient context for a practical information need.

Question (4/5): What factors did Pakistan identify as keeping Afghanistan in perpetual instability?

Answer (ok): Inadequate international support for economic rehabilitation and for extending security coverage beyond the capital had been a critical factor. The continuing drug problem and the presence of regional warlords had kept Afghanistan in a state of perpetual instability.

Useful attributed causal assessment, but the period matters for interpreting Afghanistan’s instability.

un · 2006/a/c_5/60/sr_64#14 · semantic

In this 2006 Fifth Committee target, Argentina promises constructive consultations on UNOTIL resources; Pakistan credits UNAMA with specified support, agrees it needs strengthening, and attributes Afghanistan’s instability to inadequate rehabilitation/security support, drugs and warlords. These are delegations’ statements, not adopted Committee decisions; concrete strengthening measures appear only later in context.

un · 2005/a/res/60/47#0 · lookup

Target is the opening of General Assembly resolution 60/47, adopted 8 December 2005: it recalls the 2002 request for an Antarctic Treaty Consultative Parties-based report, identifies Antarctica’s international significance, and reaffirms Charter-consistent management and international cooperation. These preambular positions are substantive and answerable, without implying a newly enacted detailed regulatory scheme.

Target source text
Resolution adopted by the General Assembly on 8 December 2005
[on the report of the First Committee (A/60/454)]
60/47. Question of Antarctica
The General Assembly,
Recalling its resolution 57/51 of 22 November 2002, in which it requested the Secretary-General to submit a report consisting of the information provided by the Antarctic Treaty Consultative Parties on their consultative meetings, on their activities in Antarctica and on developments in relation to Antarctica,
Taking into account the debates on the question of Antarctica held since its thirty-eighth session,
Conscious of the particular significance of Antarctica to the international community, including for international peace and security, the global and regional environment, its effects on global and regional climate conditions, and scientific research,
Reaffirming that the management and use of Antarctica should be conducted in accordance with the purposes and principles of the Charter of the United Nations and in the interest of maintaining international peace and security and of promoting international cooperation for the benefit of mankind as a whole,

Reviewer preference: simple, revised. Both nonempty sets are clear, supported and substantively equivalent in quality.

simple

All three useful as written; preambular status does not make them invalid.

Question (5/5): According to the General Assembly's resolution adopted on 8 December 2005 on the question of Antarctica, what did it recall resolution 57/51 had requested?

Answer (ok): Recalling its resolution 57/51 of 22 November 2002, in which it requested the Secretary-General to submit a report consisting of the information provided by the Antarctic Treaty Consultative Parties on their consultative meetings, on their activities in Antarctica and on developments in relation to Antarctica,

Clearly distinguishes the recalled 2002 request from the 2005 resolution and asks its substance.

Question (5/5): According to the General Assembly's resolution adopted on 8 December 2005 on the question of Antarctica, what particular significance did it recognize Antarctica as having?

Answer (ok): Conscious of the particular significance of Antarctica to the international community, including for international peace and security, the global and regional environment, its effects on global and regional climate conditions, and scientific research,

Natural question about the interests making Antarctica significant, with source clearly identified.

Question (5/5): According to the General Assembly's resolution adopted on 8 December 2005 on the question of Antarctica, what did it reaffirm about the management and use of Antarctica?

Answer (ok): Reaffirming that the management and use of Antarctica should be conducted in accordance with the purposes and principles of the Charter of the United Nations and in the interest of maintaining international peace and security and of promoting international cooperation for the benefit of mankind as a whole,

Clear request for the Assembly’s reaffirmed management principles.

revised

All three useful as written, covering the same main substantive points.

Question (5/5): On 8 December 2005, which matters did the General Assembly cite as contributing to Antarctica’s particular significance in the resolution concerning the Question of Antarctica?

Answer (ok): international peace and security, the global and regional environment, its effects on global and regional climate conditions, and scientific research

Useful source-specific question about the listed dimensions of Antarctica’s importance.

Question (5/5): On 8 December 2005, what did the General Assembly reaffirm about the management and use of Antarctica in the resolution concerning the Question of Antarctica?

Answer (ok): that the management and use of Antarctica should be conducted in accordance with the purposes and principles of the Charter of the United Nations and in the interest of maintaining international peace and security and of promoting international cooperation for the benefit of mankind as a whole

Clear and accurately qualified reaffirmation question.

Question (5/5): On 8 December 2005, what report content did the General Assembly recall requesting in the resolution concerning the Question of Antarctica?

Answer (ok): a report consisting of the information provided by the Antarctic Treaty Consultative Parties on their consultative meetings, on their activities in Antarctica and on developments in relation to Antarctica

Specific report-content question that preserves the historical recall.

original

Abstention; no question-quality rating.

un · 2013/a/res/68/143#7 · lookup

Target paragraphs 12–13 of the 2013 African refugee-assistance resolution recall registration responsibilities, describe harassment without documentation and protection benefits, request UNHCR help where States cannot register, and call for action and generous contributions. Requests and responsibilities must remain distinct from proof of actual State incapacity or implemented programs.

Target source text
12. Recalls the conclusion on registration of refugees and asylum seekers adopted by the Executive Committee of the Programme of the High Commissioner at its fifty-second session, notes the many forms of harassment faced by refugees and asylum seekers who remain without any form of documentation attesting to their status, recalls the responsibility of States to register refugees on their territories and, as appropriate, the responsibility of the Office of the High Commissioner or mandated international bodies to do so, reiterates in this context the central role that early and effective registration and documentation can play, guided by protection considerations, in enhancing protection and supporting efforts to find durable solutions, and calls upon the Office, as appropriate, to help States to conduct this procedure should they be unable to register refugees on their territory;
13. Calls upon the international community, including States and the Office of the High Commissioner and other relevant United Nations organizations, within their respective mandates, to take concrete action to meet the protection and assistance needs of refugees, returnees and displaced persons and to contribute generously to projects and programmes aimed at alleviating their plight, facilitating durable solutions for refugees and displaced persons and supporting vulnerable local host communities;

Reviewer preference: original, simple, revised. All nonempty sets are strong; A’s single candidate limits coverage but not its question quality.

original

One strong conditional question.

Question (5/5): Under the 2013 General Assembly resolution on African refugee assistance, when should the Office of the High Commissioner help States register refugees?

Answer (ok): should they be unable to register refugees on their territory

Clear conditional assistance question with source, subject and year identified.

simple

Three clear, well-located substantive questions.

Question (5/5): In paragraph 12 of the General Assembly’s 2013 resolution on assistance to refugees in Africa, what registration responsibilities and conditional assistance are specified?

Answer (ok): recalls the responsibility of States to register refugees on their territories and, as appropriate, the responsibility of the Office of the High Commissioner or mandated international bodies to do so, reiterates in this context the central role that early and effective registration and documentation can play, guided by protection considerations, in enhancing protection and supporting efforts to find durable solutions, and calls upon the Office, as appropriate, to help States to conduct this procedure should they be unable to register refugees on their territory;

Useful coherent question about the allocation of responsibility and conditional help, precisely located.

Question (5/5): In paragraph 13 of the General Assembly’s 2013 resolution on assistance to refugees in Africa, what action is requested from the international community and relevant organizations?

Answer (ok): Calls upon the international community, including States and the Office of the High Commissioner and other relevant United Nations organizations, within their respective mandates, to take concrete action to meet the protection and assistance needs of refugees, returnees and displaced persons and to contribute generously to projects and programmes aimed at alleviating their plight, facilitating durable solutions for refugees and displaced persons and supporting vulnerable local host communities;

Clearly asks the requested international response, preserving its requested status.

Question (5/5): What role does paragraph 12 of the General Assembly’s 2013 resolution on assistance to refugees in Africa assign to early registration and documentation?

Answer (ok): reiterates in this context the central role that early and effective registration and documentation can play, guided by protection considerations, in enhancing protection and supporting efforts to find durable solutions

Natural policy-purpose question about registration and documentation in the identified paragraph.

revised

Three clear and focused substantive questions.

Question (5/5): What did the General Assembly's 2013 resolution on assistance to refugees, returnees and displaced persons in Africa call upon the international community to contribute toward?

Answer (ok): projects and programmes aimed at alleviating their plight, facilitating durable solutions for refugees and displaced persons and supporting vulnerable local host communities

Clear request for the purposes of contributions under an identified refugee-assistance resolution.

un · 2007/a/res/62/137#12 · lookup

Paragraph12 of the December2007 Beijing follow-up resolution urges Governments, UN-system entities and civil society to integrate gender perspectives in implementation/follow-up and consider them during preparation. It names examples in2007–2008, but provides no concrete mechanism for changing event design and does not impose a binding duty.

un · 2013/a/res/68/18#1 · lookup

This2013 resolution concerns graduation from the least-developed-country category, not educational graduation or an unspecified income category. It grants Equatorial Guinea six extra months and Vanuatu one extra year before the standard three-year preparation. It invites a national smooth-transition strategy with support from the UN system collectively, without naming a particular UN agency. ECOSOC endorses the Committee recommendation; the Assembly takes note.

Target source text
1. Reaffirms that graduating from the category of least developed countries should not result in a disruption of development plans, programmes and projects;
2. Takes note of the endorsement by the Economic and Social Council of the recommendation of the Committee for Development Policy that Equatorial Guinea be graduated from the least developed country category, and decides to provide Equatorial Guinea, on an exceptional basis, with an additional preparatory period of six months before the start of the three-year preparatory period leading to graduation;
3. Invites Equatorial Guinea to prepare, during the three and a half year period between the adoption of the present resolution and its graduation from the least developed country category, its national smooth-transition strategy, with the support of the United Nations system and in cooperation with its bilateral and multilateral development and trading partners;
4. Takes note of the endorsement by the Council of the recommendation of the Committee that Vanuatu be graduated from the least developed country category, and decides to provide Vanuatu, on an exceptional basis, with an additional preparatory period of one year before the start of the three-year preparatory period leading to graduation;

Reviewer preference: revised. C has no material premise defect and clearly distinguishes additional from standard preparation. B's entity question asks for information absent from the target.

simple

Useful questions with a recurring omission of what country graduation means here.

Answer (minor): Invites Equatorial Guinea to prepare, during the three and a half year period between the adoption of the present resolution and its graduation from the least developed country category, its national smooth-transition strategy, with the support of the United Nations system and in cooperation with its bilateral and multilateral development and trading partners;

A useful planning-task question with country and resolution year; naming the least-developed-country category would clarify graduation, and the answer includes extra timing/partner detail.

Question (4/5): What did the General Assembly's 2013 resolution on graduation reaffirm about disruption of development plans, programmes and projects?

Answer (ok): Reaffirms that graduating from the category of least developed countries should not result in a disruption of development plans, programmes and projects;

The continuity issue is clear, but the graduation category is left implicit in the question.

Question (4/5): What exceptional preparatory timing did the General Assembly's 2013 resolution provide Equatorial Guinea before its graduation process?

Answer (minor): Takes note of the endorsement by the Economic and Social Council of the recommendation of the Committee for Development Policy that Equatorial Guinea be graduated from the least developed country category, and decides to provide Equatorial Guinea, on an exceptional basis, with an additional preparatory period of six months before the start of the three-year preparatory period leading to graduation;

A substantive timing question, although graduation process is vague and the category is unstated; the answer adds unasked endorsement history.

A useful, well-identified question about the exceptional transition arrangement, without asserting implementation or an unsupported reason; the answer adds endorsement history.

original

The2014 Yemen resolution assigns the sanctions committee information-review, designation, guidelines, reporting, dialogue and alleged-violation tasks. The initial report is due within60days, followed by reports as the committee deems necessary. The target establishes a mandate; it does not document that meetings or enforcement actions actually took place. Target source text
(b) To seek and review information regarding those individuals and entities who may be engaging in the acts described in paragraph 17 and 18 above;
(c) To designate individuals and entities to be subject to the measures imposed in paragraphs 11 and 15 above;

(e) To report within 60 days to the Security Council on its work and thereafter to report as deemed necessary by the Committee;
(f) To encourage a dialogue between the Committee and interested Member States, in particular those in the region, including by inviting representatives of such States to meet with the Committee to discuss implementation of the measures;
(g) To seek from all States whatever information it may consider useful regarding the actions taken by them to implement effectively the measures imposed;
(h) To examine and take appropriate action on information regarding alleged violations or non-compliance with the measures contained in paragraphs 11 and 15;

The annual Working Group report dated September 2012 describes a May 2012 discussion of Sri Lanka, drawing on a December 2011 Secretary-General report. It reports child reintegration/tracing progress, a tracing plan, remaining TMVP accountability concerns and expert negotiation of draft conclusions.

Target source text
(b) Progress had been achieved in separating children from Tamil Makkal Viduthalai Pulikal (TMVP) and Liberation Tigers of Tamil Eelam (LTTE) and reintegrating them into society, and in tracing children who had been separated from their parents in the final stages of the conflict.
In particular, the Government was developing a plan to undertake tracing activities in hospitals, children's homes and police posts through the country;
(c) However, additional measures were needed to further such efforts.
Concerns remained regarding the need to establish accountability for the recruitment and use of children by remnants of TMVP.
14. The Deputy Permanent Representative of Sri Lanka presented the views of his Government.
The Working Group then held an exchange of views and, following the discussion, the experts were given the task of negotiating draft conclusions of the Group that would provide concrete follow-up to the Secretary-General's report.
15. The main elements of the statement made by the Deputy Permanent Representative of Sri Lanka, as well as the exchange of views among the members of the Working Group, will be reflected in the conclusions.

un · 2007/cedaw/c/sr_770_b_#18 · lookup

A Colombian delegate at the January 2007 CEDAW review describes existing support for women heads of household and private-sector education. Business leaders would be required to eliminate salary discrimination; the text does not say that requirement was already implemented.

Target source text
The next step was to coordinate with the various agencies responsible for providing resources and implementing the Plan.
40. The number of women heads of household in Colombia was growing, and they enjoyed special protection under various laws.
While 80 per cent of male heads of household had a companion, most female heads of household lived alone, and therefore bore a heavier burden, both in terms of income level and in terms of childcare and household chores.
Affirmative action measures had therefore been introduced to protect such women, including access to credit and to housing subsidies.
In the private sector, the Government promoted parity through education aimed at helping women achieve decision-making and management positions.
For example, more and more women were taking jobs in the financial sector.
The Government had studied the issue of gender-based salary gaps in cooperation with business and industry leaders, who would be required to eliminate such discrimination.

un · 2010/ccpr/c/sr_2695#25 · lookup

The Human Rights Committee is revising reporting guidelines on 15 March 2010. Keller supports deletion/revision but retains an example for nonexperts; the Chair proposes moving military-service guidance to article 18, with apparent agreement. Proposals and drafting agreement should not automatically become implemented policy.

Target source text
79. Mr. Rivas Posada agreed that paragraph 57 should be deleted.
80. Ms. Keller said that she endorsed the suggestion to delete paragraph 57 and add a fourth bullet in paragraph 54.
However, given that the document was addressed not just to human rights experts, in her view the phrase "such as abduction of women and children" should not be deleted.
Perhaps it could be included in parentheses, after the word "servitude".
81. The Chair said that just a few days earlier, Uzbekistan had referred to alternative military service under article 8.
Therefore, for the purpose of informing States how the issue was to be dealt with, perhaps paragraph 57 should contain a reference to article 18, and the present paragraph 57 should be moved to the section covering article 18.
It appeared that the members of the Committee agreed with that approach, as they did with the new version of paragraph 54.

Reviewer preference: simple, revised. A and B are consistently strong source-based drafting questions. B's answer-splicing defect does not make its question worse.

simple

Both questions are useful and accurately framed as reported drafting positions.

Question (5/5): According to the Human Rights Committee’s 15 March 2010 summary record on revising paragraphs 54 and 57, what did Ms. Keller say about retaining “such as abduction of women and children”?

Answer (minor): Ms. Keller said that she endorsed the suggestion to delete paragraph 57 and add a fourth bullet in paragraph 54. However, given that the document was addressed not just to human rights experts, in her view the phrase "such as abduction of women and children" should not be deleted. Perhaps it could be included in parentheses, after the word "servitude".

A clear source-specific question about a meaningful drafting choice; the answer also includes unrelated paragraph-deletion and bullet changes.

Question (5/5): According to the Human Rights Committee’s 15 March 2010 summary record on revising paragraphs 54 and 57, what did the Chair propose after Uzbekistan referred to alternative military service under article 8?

Answer (ok): The Chair said that just a few days earlier, Uzbekistan had referred to alternative military service under article 8. Therefore, for the purpose of informing States how the issue was to be dealt with, perhaps paragraph 57 should contain a reference to article 18, and the present paragraph 57 should be moved to the section covering article 18.

Identifies the precise committee episode and asks what the Chair proposed, without treating the proposal as enacted policy.

revised

Three strong, specific questions; one answer needs grammatical repair, without reducing question quality.

Question (5/5): According to the Human Rights Committee's 15 March 2010 summary record, why should the phrase “such as abduction of women and children” be retained, and where might it be placed?

Answer (ok): However, given that the document was addressed not just to human rights experts, in her view the phrase "such as abduction of women and children" should not be deleted. Perhaps it could be included in parentheses, after the word "servitude".

The rationale and proposed placement form one coherent drafting question, directly supported by the block.

Question (5/5): According to the Human Rights Committee's 15 March 2010 summary record, how did the Chair propose revising and relocating paragraph 57 regarding alternative military service?

Answer (ok): Therefore, for the purpose of informing States how the issue was to be dealt with, perhaps paragraph 57 should contain a reference to article 18, and the present paragraph 57 should be moved to the section covering article 18.

Clear question about both linked elements of the proposed revision, with the committee and meeting identified.

Question (5/5): According to the Human Rights Committee's 15 March 2010 summary record, what changes did Ms. Keller endorse concerning paragraph 57 and paragraph 54?

Answer (minor): that paragraph 57 should be deleted and add a fourth bullet in paragraph 54

A sound question about endorsed changes; the answer splices its wording into an ungrammatical deleted-and-add construction.

original

Both are usable confirmation questions with avoidable wording imprecision.

Question (4/5): In 2010, did Ms. Keller oppose deleting paragraph 54's reference to abduction of women and children under the 2010 Human Rights Committee summary record?

Answer (ok): in her view the phrase "such as abduction of women and children" should not be deleted

un · 2000/cd/pv_841#11 · lookup

Mr Soutar speaks for the UK at the Conference on Disarmament on 10 February 2000. The block describes a coordination/work-programme option and argues that an FMCT provides confidence that no new weapons fissile material can be produced. Such an assurance alone is not a complete answer to what assurance. Five years refers to an unfulfilled objective, not necessarily the date of the subsequent consultations.

Target source text
In your subsequent consultations, you were to bring forward for consideration a number of options, one of which would have permitted the appointment of special coordinators to deal with the issues of nuclear disarmament and the prevention of an arms race in outer space, while allowing the Conference to agree on a work programme, including the establishment of an ad hoc committee for negotiations on an FMCT.
Five years on, that common objective remains unfulfilled. The importance of an FMCT has not diminished in the intervening period. Indeed, events in the closing years of the last century have underlined its necessity.
In our view, it remains the case that before there can be an effective and verifiable ban on nuclear weapons globally, there must be confidence that no new fissile material for such weapons can be produced.
An FMCT would deliver such an assurance, and in so doing, put in place a vital foundation for the achievement of global nuclear disarmament.

Reviewer preference: revised. B provides consistently clear and substantive questions. Its incomplete assurance answer is recorded separately and does not reduce question-set preference.

simple

Two strong questions and one broader option question; the first has a serious answer-span defect only.

Question (5/5): On 10 February 2000, according to the United Kingdom representative’s plenary statement to the Conference on Disarmament, what assurance would an FMCT provide?

Answer (major): An FMCT would deliver such an assurance, and in so doing, put in place a vital foundation for the achievement of global nuclear disarmament.

A clear and useful attributed assurance question; the supplied answer says such an assurance without including what that assurance is.

Question (4/5): On 10 February 2000, according to the United Kingdom representative’s plenary statement to the Conference on Disarmament, what option was to be considered?

Answer (ok): One of which would have permitted the appointment of special coordinators to deal with the issues of nuclear disarmament and the prevention of an arms race in outer space, while allowing the Conference to agree on a work programme, including the establishment of an ad hoc committee for negotiations on an FMCT.

The dated UK speech identifies the source, but what option was to be considered is broad without naming the work-programme issue.

Question (5/5): On 10 February 2000, according to the United Kingdom representative’s plenary statement to the Conference on Disarmament, what must precede an effective, verifiable global nuclear-weapons ban?

Answer (ok): In our view, it remains the case that before there can be an effective and verifiable ban on nuclear weapons globally, there must be confidence that no new fissile material for such weapons can be produced.

A precise, attributed question about the UK's stated prerequisite for a nuclear-weapons ban.

revised

All three questions are substantive and well identified. The third answer is incomplete, not a weak question.

Question (5/5): What option did the United Kingdom describe on 10 February 2000 in its Conference on Disarmament statement as combining special coordinators with a work programme?

Answer (ok): In your subsequent consultations, you were to bring forward for consideration a number of options, one of which would have permitted the appointment of special coordinators to deal with the issues of nuclear disarmament and the prevention of an arms race in outer space, while allowing the Conference to agree on a work programme, including the establishment of an ad hoc committee for negotiations on an FMCT.

The question identifies the diplomatic option and episode while leaving its substantive elements unknown.

Question (5/5): What condition did the United Kingdom identify on 10 February 2000 in its Conference on Disarmament statement as preceding an effective, verifiable global ban on nuclear weapons?

Clear attribution, date and substantive prerequisite; directly answerable in the target.

Question (5/5): What assurance and broader disarmament role did the United Kingdom attribute to an FMCT on 10 February 2000 in its Conference on Disarmament statement?

A coherent question linking the assurance to its wider purpose; the answer fails to spell out the assurance behind such an assurance.

Answer (ok): that no new fissile material for such weapons can be produced

The attributed prerequisite is useful, but the long confidence-did-require construction is less natural than asking what confidence was necessary.

Question (4/5): In the 2000 Conference on Disarmament meeting record on FMCT, did the United Kingdom consider the importance of an FMCT diminished?

A legitimate confirmation question about the UK's stated position, though less informative than asking its rationale.

The December 2005 armaments-transparency resolution concerns the UN Register of Conventional Arms. It recalls a request for Member State views and asks the Secretary-General, assisted by experts convened in 2006 under resource/representation constraints, to report toward a sixty-first-session decision. Target source text
4. Reaffirms its decision, with a view to further development of the Register, to keep the scope of and participation in the Register under review and, to that end:
(a) Recalls its request to Member States to provide the Secretary-General with their views on the continuing operation of the Register and its further development and on transparency measures related to weapons of mass destruction;
(b) Requests the Secretary-General, with the assistance of a group of governmental experts to be convened in 2006, within available resources, on the basis of equitable geographical representation, to prepare a report on the continuing operation of the Register and its further development, taking into account the work of the Conference on Disarmament, the views expressed by Member States and the reports of the Secretary-General on the continuing operation of the Register and its further development, with a view to taking a decision at its sixty-first session;

Played in 2006 treats the planned consideration of views as an observed process; the answer also omits the report frame explaining where the input was to be used.

Question (5/5): How did the General Assembly arrange a fresh assessment of the United Nations Register of Conventional Arms in 2005?

Answer (ok): Requests the Secretary-General, with the assistance of a group of governmental experts to be convened in 2006, within available resources, on the basis of equitable geographical representation, to prepare a report on the continuing operation of the Register and its further development

Natural question about the mechanism for a fresh assessment, correctly situated at the 2005 decision.

revised

A good process question paired with a thin input question.

Question (4/5): What review process did the General Assembly set in motion in December 2005 to guide a decision on the Register at its sixty-first session?

Answer (ok): Requests the Secretary-General, with the assistance of a group of governmental experts to be convened in 2006, within available resources, on the basis of equitable geographical representation, to prepare a report on the continuing operation of the Register and its further development, taking into account the work of the Conference on Disarmament, the views expressed by Member States and the reports of the Secretary-General on the continuing operation of the Register and its further development, with a view to taking a decision at its sixty-first session;

A useful review-process question with date and intended session; expanding the Register's name would make it self-contained.

Answer (ok): Recalls its request to Member States to provide the Secretary-General with their views on the continuing operation of the Register and its further development and on transparency measures related to weapons of mass destruction;

The question already names the requested future-development and transparency topics, leaving little additional information in the answer.

un · 2013/a/res/67/145#7 · lookup

The preambular passage identifies gender-specific disadvantages, requests CSW consideration within the 2013 theme, and endorses cooperation. It identifies no specific countries for trafficking demand and no detailed protective interventions.

Target source text
Noting that some of the demand for prostitution and forced labour is met by trafficking in persons in some parts of the world,
Acknowledging that women and girl victims of trafficking, on account of their gender, are further disadvantaged and marginalized by a general lack of information on or awareness and recognition of their human rights and by the stigmatization often associated with trafficking, as well as by the obstacles they meet in gaining access to accurate information and recourse mechanisms in cases of violation of their rights, and that special measures are required for their protection and to increase their awareness,
Encouraging the Commission on the Status of Women at its fiftyseventh session to consider the issue of trafficking in women and girls within the framework of the priority theme for 2013, "Elimination and prevention of all forms of violence against women and girls",
Reaffirming the importance of bilateral, subregional, regional and international cooperation mechanisms and initiatives, including information exchanges on best practices, of Governments and intergovernmental and nongovernmental organizations to address the problem of trafficking in persons, especially women and children,

Reviewer preference: simple. A has the more consistently answerable set; C contains a measures question whose requested detail is absent.

simple

All questions are source-specific; the barriers/cooperation questions add more information than the theme question.

Question (4/5): According to the General Assembly resolution adopted on 20 December 2012 concerning trafficking in women and girls, why are women and girl victims further disadvantaged, and what measures are required?

Answer (ok): Acknowledging that women and girl victims of trafficking, on account of their gender, are further disadvantaged and marginalized by a general lack of information on or awareness and recognition of their human rights and by the stigmatization often associated with trafficking, as well as by the obstacles they meet in gaining access to accurate information and recourse mechanisms in cases of violation of their rights, and that special measures are required for their protection and to increase their awareness

Accurately scopes the resolution and connects barriers with protection needs. Asking what measures are required is slightly broader than the source, which gives their purposes rather than concrete interventions.

Question (5/5): According to the General Assembly resolution adopted on 20 December 2012 concerning trafficking in women and girls, what cooperation mechanisms and initiatives does it reaffirm as important?

Answer (ok): Reaffirming the importance of bilateral, subregional, regional and international cooperation mechanisms and initiatives, including information exchanges on best practices, of Governments and intergovernmental and nongovernmental organizations to address the problem of trafficking in persons, especially women and children

Clearly asks the resolution for cooperation levels, participating actors and initiatives; the target supplies these plus best-practice exchanges.

Question (4/5): According to the General Assembly resolution adopted on 20 December 2012 concerning trafficking in women and girls, what does it encourage the Commission on the Status of Women to consider within the 2013 priority theme?

Answer (ok): Encouraging the Commission on the Status of Women at its fiftyseventh session to consider the issue of trafficking in women and girls within the framework of the priority theme for 2013, "Elimination and prevention of all forms of violence against women and girls"

Identifies the source, CSW and 2013 framework. The title already supplies much of the trafficking topic, although the answer adds its relation to the priority theme.

original

No questions generated; no numerical quality penalty.

revised

Cooperation is strong; the special-measures question implies more substantive detail than the passage provides.

Question (4/5): What did the General Assembly encourage the Commission on the Status of Women to consider at its fifty-seventh session in its resolution adopted on 20 December 2012 on trafficking in women and girls?

Answer (ok): the issue of trafficking in women and girls within the framework of the priority theme for 2013, "Elimination and prevention of all forms of violence against women and girls"

The CSW session and resolution are precise. The question partly gives away the trafficking issue through the source title, but the answer adds the 2013 priority-theme relationship.

Question (5/5): What cooperation mechanisms and initiatives did the General Assembly reaffirm as important in its resolution adopted on 20 December 2012 on trafficking in women and girls?

Answer (ok): bilateral, subregional, regional and international cooperation mechanisms and initiatives, including information exchanges on best practices, of Governments and intergovernmental and nongovernmental organizations

A focused, well-scoped question about cooperation mechanisms and initiatives. The answer supplies cooperation levels, participating actors and the expressly included best-practice information exchanges.

Question (3/5): What special measures did the General Assembly say were required for women and girl victims of trafficking in its resolution adopted on 20 December 2012 on trafficking in women and girls?

Answer (major): special measures are required for their protection and to increase their awareness

Asks which special measures were required, but the passage only calls for unspecified measures for protection and awareness; it supplies no actual measures.

Answer (ok): Acknowledging that women and girl victims of trafficking, on account of their gender, are further disadvantaged and marginalized by a general lack of information on or awareness and recognition of their human rights and by the stigmatization often associated with trafficking, as well as by the obstacles they meet in gaining access to accurate information and recourse mechanisms in cases of violation of their rights, and that special measures are required for their protection and to increase their awareness,

A supported why question: the listed information barriers, stigma and difficulty accessing remedies explain the need for special measures.

Question (3/5): Why does the resolution reaffirm the importance of bilateral, subregional, regional and international cooperation mechanisms and initiatives in the 20 December 2012 resolution?

Answer (minor): Reaffirming the importance of bilateral, subregional, regional and international cooperation mechanisms and initiatives, including information exchanges on best practices, of Governments and intergovernmental and nongovernmental organizations to address the problem of trafficking in persons, especially women and children,

The date is present but the trafficking issue is omitted and the long list of cooperation levels dominates the wording. The answer gives a broad purpose rather than an explanatory rationale.

un · 2013/a/res/67/199#12 · lookup

The target links debt sustainability with growth/development, describes typical debt-crisis harms, lists financial-system reform topics, and states objectives of crisis responses. It does not identify particular response initiatives or give a detailed causal mechanism.

Target source text
24. Also emphasizes that debt sustainability is essential for underpinning growth, underlining in this regard the importance of debt sustainability and effective debt management to the efforts to achieve national development goals, including the Millennium Development Goals, and acknowledges that sovereign debt crises tend to be costly and disruptive, including for employment and productive investments, and tend to be followed by cuts in public spending, including on health and education, affecting, in particular, the poor and vulnerable;
25. Stresses that the financial and economic crisis has highlighted the need for reform as well as added new impetus to ongoing international discussions on the reform of the international financial system and architecture, including on issues related to mandate, scope, governance, responsiveness and development orientation, as appropriate, and in this regard encourages continued open, inclusive and transparent dialogue;
26. Notes the important efforts undertaken nationally, regionally and internationally to respond to the challenges posed by the financial and economic crisis in order to ensure a full return to growth with quality jobs, to reform and strengthen financial systems and to create strong, sustainable and balanced global growth;

Reviewer preference: revised. A narrows to actual objectives and stated recommendations; B includes an unanswerable request for particular efforts.

un · 2004/s/res/1565_2004_#11 · lookup

Paragraphs 19–20 condemn DRC abuses, demand justice/civilian protection, require cooperation and access for MONUC/UN personnel, and request prompt reporting of non-compliance. Surrounding context confirms adoption on 1 October 2004.

Target source text
19. Strongly condemns violence and other violations of international humanitarian law and human rights, in particular those perpetrated against civilians, in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, and demands that all parties and Governments concerned in the region, including the Government of National Unity and Transition, take without delay all necessary steps to bring to justice those responsible for these violations and to ensure respect for human rights and international humanitarian law, as appropriate with relevant international assistance, as well as to guarantee the security and well-being of the civilian population;
20. Demands that all parties cooperate fully with the operations of MONUC and that they ensure the safety of as well as unhindered and immediate access for United Nations and associated personnel in carrying out their mandate, throughout the territory of the Democratic Republic of the Congo, demands in particular that all parties provide full access to MONUC military observers, including in all ports, airports, airfields, military bases and border crossings, and requests the Secretary-General to report without delay any failure to comply with these demands;

Reviewer preference: original. C targets discrete useful information without losing mission/episode context; A has one under-anchored question and B is unnecessarily cumbersome.

revised

Two strong MONUC questions; A1 needs a country or episode anchor.

Answer (ok): take without delay all necessary steps to bring to justice those responsible for these violations and to ensure respect for human rights and international humanitarian law, as appropriate with relevant international assistance, as well as to guarantee the security and well-being of the civilian population

Parties and regional Governments are left without a country, region or MONUC reference. A Security Council month alone does not identify the violation episode.

Answer (ok): to report without delay any failure to comply with these demands

Answer (ok): that all parties cooperate fully with the operations of MONUC and that they ensure the safety of as well as unhindered and immediate access for United Nations and associated personnel in carrying out their mandate, throughout the territory of the Democratic Republic of the Congo

Identifies MONUC, UN personnel, the Council and date, and asks a coherent cooperation/access question.
